#### INTRODUCTION

- 1 The Maintenance manuals are intended for the personnel in charge of technical work on the equipment.
- 2 The Maintenance manuals contain:
- all the technical information concerning the description and operation of the various component systems of the aircraft as well as of those equipment items whose documentation is incorporated;
  - all information necessary to perform technical work involved in :
    - servicing
    - trouble shooting
    - removal-disassembly
    - inspection-maintenance-adjustment
    - reconditioning and storage,

which may be carried out by the users. As such, they establish the limits of the maintenance authorized at organization level.

- 3 These Maintenance manuals and the related sets of work cards form an integral part of the aircraft operation documentation. Therefore, it will be necessary to refer to:
  - the Illustrated Parts List for identifying assemblies, subassemblies and detail parts;
- individual documentation of equipment as concerns function and layout of components as well as maintenance operations to be carried out on these equipment after removal from aircraft.
- 4 This maintenance documentation is made of 17 manuals. Some of these manuals cover the <u>aircraft</u> systems and structure according to a breakdown to homogeneous classes of functions; other manuals deal with general information and with specific information such as maintenance periodicities, servicing instructions, structural repairs.

Each manual is divided into sections and each section deals with a functional part or system.

This breakdown is shown in the «General Index of Maintenance Manuals».

#### PREPARATION AND USE OF MAINTENANCE MANUALS

#### 1 - BREAKDOWN OF A MAINTENANCE MANUAL

Each manual comprises:

- A A title page identifying the document.
- B A list of effective pages which is the record of all the pages making up the manual. It determines the breakdown of the manual.
- C A list of modifications incorporated in the manual. This list is specific to each manual and records only those modifications which affect the manual concerned.
- D A table of contents which lists the sections making up the manual.
- E The various sections making up the manual.

#### 2 - BREAKDOWN OF A MAINTENANCE MANUAL SECTION

Two different kinds of «sections» can be defined in a Maintenance manual dealing with an aircraft system.

#### A - Section «0» - «General Information»

This section has an «as required» structure and deals with all the matters which are common to the other sections of the manual. It gives the general maintenance methods and instructions not dealt with in work cards.

#### B - Common sections in a manual dealing with an aircraft system

Each of these sections is subdivided into 9 sub-sections which are assigned an unchanging title and sequence number :

| Sub-section<br>No. | Sub-section title                                      |
|--------------------|--------------------------------------------------------|
| 0                  | - General                                              |
| 1                  | <ul> <li>Description and operation</li> </ul>          |
| 2                  | - Trouble shooting                                     |
| 3                  | - Removal-Installation                                 |
| 4                  | - Disassembly-Assembly &                               |
| 5                  | - Inspection-Check-Adjustment                          |
| 6                  | - Cleaning-Corrosion preventive treatment-<br>Painting |
| 7                  | - Storage - Preservation - Transportation              |
| 8                  | - Repair                                               |

In each sub-section, the description of maintenance operations is dealt with in the form of a set of work cards. Each set corresponds to the manual dealing with the system and is issued separately.

{21}------------------------------------------------

![](_page_21_Picture_1.jpeg)

NOTE:

(1) Maintenance and lubrication are covered by sub-section 6.

(2) In each manual, sub-section 7 deals with preservation-packaging and transportation matters relating to the individual items of the system to which the manual is applicable and which form the subject of an incorporated documentation. As concerns the complete aircraft, these matters are dealt with in the «GENERAL» manual.

(3) Sub-section 8 covers the reconditioning operations for the items of the system to which the Manual is devoted. The structural repairs of the aircraft form the subject of a separate Manual:

«STRUCTURAL REPAIRS».

(4) Any sub-section which is not applicable is not shown.

#### 3 - ARRANGEMENT OF A COMMON SECTION IN A MANUAL DEALING WITH A SYSTEM

#### A — Sub-section 0 - GENERAL

This is an introductory sub-section which gives the general principles of the functional system covered by the section. A schematic diagram is usually attached to this sub-section.

#### B - Sub-section 1 - DESCRIPTION - OPERATION

This sub-section is arranged as follows:

(1) A table of components lists all the units, accessories and equipment items of the system concerned, in the logical descriptive sequence. It gives the essential characteristics of these equipment items, their function in the system concerned, their location within the aircraft, and it indicates whether the relevant documentation is separate or incorporated.

Layout illustrations are usually attached to this sub-section, which also refers to the functional diagram.

The purpose of the table of components is to clarify the system description, by specifying the purpose and characteristics of the various components.

In this table, the units, accessories or equipment items are identified by :

- a symbol index number for those equipment items which have an electrical function,

- a sequential index number (1 to X) when the item is not assigned any reference designation.

Note that the values shown in the table of components are <u>rated values</u>. When tolerances apply to these values, they are always given in the sub-section which deals with maintenance.

(2) A description of the system operation.

This refers to the functional diagram.

NOTE: The detailed description and operation of the individual equipment items are given in their separate documentation or, if applicable, in the «incorporated documentation» (see below).

(3) The description-operation part of the «incorporated documentation» for some equipment items is contained in pages or groups of pages which are independent and placed next to the description and operation of the system to which they belong.

{22}------------------------------------------------

![](_page_22_Picture_1.jpeg)

The «incorporated documentation» is given in the same order as it appears in the table of components; it is illustrated whenever necessary and the internal index numbers are given in the sequence 1 to X for each component, independently of the index numbers shown in the table of components.

#### C — Sub-section 2 - TROUBLE SHOOTING

This sub-section shows, in the form of a synopsis, the methods to be applied to determine the causes of possible malfunctions.

#### D - Sub-sections 3 to 8

Except for general check and maintenance methods, these sub-sections are dealt with in the form of work cards issued separately.

#### 4 - IDENTIFICATION OF INSPECTION DOORS

The figures showing the location of the inspection doors are part of manual 01 «GENERAL», to which reference should be made.

The door numbering principles are as follows:

- (a) Sub-assembly on which a door is located
  - Fuselage the number consists of four digits.
  - Wings the number consists of letter V followed by three digits.
  - Stabilator the number consists of letter E followed by three digits.
  - Fin the number consists of letter D followed by three digits.

#### (b) Fuselage doors

The first two digits show the number of the frame forward of the door; the next two digits are the clock position of the door within the fuselage section in which it is located.

- Example: fuselage door between F.29 and 30 in the middle part of the RH side: door 29-03.

#### (c) Wing doors

The first digit locates the door in relation to the rib which precedes the door as considered from the aircraft centerline to the wing tip.

The last two digits indicate in which of the following areas the door is located:

1 to 29 - leading edge

30 to 59 - center wing box

60 to 90 - trailing edge.

{23}------------------------------------------------

![](_page_23_Picture_1.jpeg)

The door numbering direction is from the wing root to the wing tip and from the leading edge to the trailing edge.

Even numbers apply to the upper surface doors and odd numbers apply to the lower surface doors.

Example: door located between ribs 1 and 2 on the upper surface within the center wing box: V 138.

#### (d) Fin doors

Same principle as for the wings.

The odd numbers apply to the LH side and the even numbers apply to the RH side.

#### (e) Stabilator doors

Same principle as for the wings.

#### 5 - DIAGRAM DRAWING RULES

There are three kinds of diagrams:

- schematic diagrams
- functional diagrams
- wiring diagrams.

#### A - Schematic diagram.

The schematic diagram is drawn in the form of a block showing the main component interconnections, and illustrating the functional principle of the system and the connections to the other systems or functions.

#### B - Functional diagram.

The functional diagram represents all the units, accessories and equipment items making up the system and their functional connections. The detailed operation of the function can be followed on that diagram.

The functional diagrams are usually shown in <u>inoperative</u> condition (i.e. no pressure, no power, aircraft on its wheels). The complex functions may be illustrated by several functional diagrams associated with the main operating phases.

The equipment inside is represented in the form of a block or symbol. For the actual equipment illustration, refer to the relevant publication.

The functional diagrams show the terminals, except for the relays, on/off switches, selector switches and indicator lights.

The functional diagrams define the type of the information conveyed. Cut-off and grounding connectors are not shown unless an aircraft boundary is concerned.

#### (1) Test connectors on equipment.

The test points are shown by the associated terminal number which is encircled and hooked to the tested circuit. If there are several test connectors, the reference designation of the connector to which the point belongs is written next to the test point.

#### (2) Aircraft test connectors.

These are shown with their terminal numbers and reference designations in the same manner as the other aircraft wiring connectors.

#### C - Wiring diagram.

The wiring diagrams form the subject of manual 16 to which reference should be made.

#### D - Symbols used in the diagrams.

The symbols used in the diagrams are shown in the following charts:

#### 6 - IDENTIFICATION OF PAGES

#### A - Each page is identified by:

- the Manual number in the top R/H corner.
- the date of issue of the page in the bottom L/H corner.
- the page number in the bottom R/H corner.
- if applicable, the technical definition of the page just on top of the date of issue. This shows the number of the modification applied.

The page number consists of two groups of digits separated by a dash:

- (1) The Section number
- (2) The page number within the Section.

This page number is made up as follows:

- a first digit shows the Sub-Section number (0 to 8).
- the next two digits show the page rank within the Sub-Section (01 to 99).
- B- The figures are assigned numbers within a Section (1 to x).

#### 7 - CROSS-REFERENCES

#### A - From text to text :

from Manual to Manual from Section to Section within a same section

Manual/Section

#### B - From text to Figure and from Figure to Figure :

from Manual to Manual: Manual/Section/Fig. (if necessary) from Section to Section: Manual/Section/Fig. (if necessary) within a same section: Figure.

#### 8 - UNITS OF MEASURE USED

The units of measure are written as follows:

«International measuring system (IMS) units (converted values, possible complementary units)».

The converted values are given in the order: British units and US units.

Whenever converted units of measure are suspect, it is mandatory to refer to the international measuring system (IMS) unit of measure.

In some specific cases, for which the units of measure are taken from standards, the units of measure remain those of the IMS international system except for US Standards where the units are US units.

In addition, characteristics specific to some equipment are given in IMS units (example : aircraft dimensions, etc.).

#### 9 - REFERENCE TO OFFICIAL DOCUMENTS

In the publications, references are made to French official documents, such as Technical Regulations (RT), Technical Circulars (CT), BNAé Standards, etc. All these documents are namely listed in Manual 01 «GENERAL».

{29}------------------------------------------------

#### **REVISION OF MAINTENANCE MANUALS**

#### 1 - PUBLICATION DEVELOPMENT PRINCIPLE

This development is performed in two steps:

- A Issue of supplements which are temporarily incorporated in the publications.
- B Actual revision which permanently incorporates information in the publications. Such information may be newly issued or previously published in supplements (replacement of pages or addition of new pages).

#### 2 - REVISION TO PUBLICATIONS

#### A - Procedure

Revision involves two operations:

- insertion of new pages and replacement of modified pages.

- systematic replacement of the title page and list of effective pages which serves as a guide to the insertion of revised pages.

#### B - Page numbering

First case - Addition of one or more new pages following the last page of a Sub-Section. The pages are numbered in the normal order.

Second case - Insertion of one or more new pages between two existing pages.

The new page(s) bears the number of the preceding page followed by a capital letter, with the exception of letters I. O and Z.

The letters are chosen in every case so as to provide for maximum further insertions unless the initial numbering sequence is changed.

The principle is illustrated in the table below:

![](_page_29_Figure_19.jpeg)

{30}------------------------------------------------

![](_page_30_Picture_1.jpeg)

#### Example:

Assuming that several pages are to be inserted in succession between pages 6 and 7. The first one will be numbered 6M, the second one 6F if it is to be placed before 6M, or 6T if it is to be placed after 6M. Similarly, if it is necessary to insert a third page later on, its number will be 6F or 6T, 6C or 6J, 6Q or 6W as applicable.

Assuming that the following are to be inserted simultaneously:

- 2 pages: they will be numbered 6H and 6R.
- 3 pages: they will be numbered 6F, 6M and 6T.

Third case - Insertion of a new page following application of an official modification. The principle consists in showing up the two states simultaneously in the publication, i.e. post- and pre-modification.

- (a) The modification can be inserted according to the principle stated above without altering the page numbering sequence: There will be a new page which supersedes the preceding one.
- (b) As a result of the insertion of both states (post- and pre-modification), if the number of pages increases, the solution will be as follows:
- both the pre- and post-modification pages will bear the same number. «MOD...X» will be shown on top of the date on the modified page (this means modification X applied). Both pages will be shown in the list of effective pages with.

Remark: These two pages may have different dates.

(c) Where a complete paragraph or a Section is deeply affected by a modification (most of the pages changed), the complete paragraph which is modified will be found first and followed by the complete paragraph which is not modified. The table of contents will list both paragraphs (or Sections).

The unmodified pages will be deleted during a further revision only when the modification has been applied to all aircraft.

C - Identification of revised text portions

The modifications to the text, insertion or deletion, are identified by a vertical line in the margin.

Those pages which are entirely changed are identified by a vertical line in the margin on a level with the date of issue.

#### DESCRIPTION OF CONTROL AND NAVIGATION SYSTEM (Figures 1, 2 and 3)

Restricted

The control and navigation system includes:

- one pitot static system sensing and distributing pitot and static pressures,
- flight instruments in the cockpit, supplied by the pitot static system :
  - standby altimeter
  - mach/airspeed indicator
  - vertical speed indicator
- independent flight instruments in the cockpit :
  - accelerometer
  - standby horizon
  - standby magnetic compass
- one angle-of-attack system measuring, distributing and displaying the aircraft angle of attack,
- one air data computer supplied by the pitot static and angle-of-attack systems, which generates and distributes the following data:
  - static pressure
  - dynamic pressure
  - pressure altitude
  - mach number
  - vertical speed
  - true airspeed
  - indicated airspeed
  - true angle of attack

the pressure altitude, indicated airspeed and mach number are displayed to the pilot on the head-up display.

- one attitude and heading system including:

- one normal channel (gyro/accelerometer center) which detects and computes the aircraft

attitude :

- transverse attitude
- longitudinal attitude
- heading
- one emergency channel which, in case of failure of the normal channel, distributes the heading information to the same associated systems.

These data are displayed to the pilot on:

- spherical indicator (transverse and longitudinal attitudes and heading)
- head-up display (transverse and longitudinal attitudes and heading)
- navigation indicator (heading)
- one radio compass system locating the aircraft in relation to a ground beacon (bearing)
- one marker beacon system signalling passage over marker beacons
- one navigation system including:
  - a Doppler radar
  - a main computer
  - a navigation control panel
  - a navigation indicator
- one radio-altimeter system measuring the height of the aircraft with respect to the ground; this information is displayed to the pilot on the head-up display.

NOTE: Figures 2 and 3 define the operating data and the use of a radio-electrical beacon.

# DESCRIPTION OF THE REFERENCE SYSTEMS USED BY THE NAVIGATION SYSTEM

#### 1 - GEOGRAPHIC AXIS

![](_page_39_Figure_5.jpeg)

- Axis ON pointing to geographic North
- Axis OE pointing to geographic East
- Axis OZo perpendicular to axes ON and OE.

#### 2 - REFERENCE AXIS

![](_page_39_Figure_10.jpeg)

Is obtained from the geographic reference by one rotation around OZo of the true heading angle TH.

#### 3 - AIRCRAFT AXIS

![](_page_39_Picture_13.jpeg)

Is obtained from the reference axis by:

- one pitch rotation P around OYo.
- one roll rotation R around OX.

### SPECIAL OPERATIONS TO BE PERFORMED AFTER INSTALLING CONTROL AND NAVIGATION SYSTEM EQUIPMENT

#### 1 - SCOPE

To describe special operations to be performed after installing or replacing an equipment item. These apply only to items, the removal/installation of which is not covered by this manual. The description of the general operations is given in the «instructions to be followed when working on the systems» (see 01-0).

#### 2 - EQUIPMENT INVOLVED

- A Standby compass
  - Perform standby compass compensation setting.
- B Standby horizon
  - Cage gyro.
  - Check Roll and Pitch parameters at acquisition unit (see 17-1).
- C Accelerometer
  - Set white moving indexes to g = 1.
- D Angle-of-attack probe
  - Check local angle-of-attack parameter at acquisition unit (see 17-1).
- E Standby altimeter
  - Select pressure corresponding to field altitude (indicated altitude 0).
- F Vertical speed indicator
  - Set pointer to zero.
- G Top flux valve
  - Ensure that flux valve and access door attaching screws are non-magnetic.
  - Perform normal heading channel compensation setting (see 12-3).
- $\mathsf{H}-\mathsf{Removable}$  compensator (on interface electronics box)
  - Perform normal heading channel compensation setting (see 12-3).

{44}------------------------------------------------

![](_page_44_Picture_0.jpeg)

- I Bottom flux valve
  - Ensure that flux valve and access door attaching screws are non-magnetic.
  - Perform emergency heading channel compensation setting (see 12-3).
- J Emergency gyromagnetic compass
  - Select local latitude.
  - Perform emergency heading channel compensation setting (see 12-3).
- K Navigation indicator
  - Check heading parameter at acquisition unit (see 17-1).
- L Radio-compass loop antenna
  - Adjust the radio-compass loop antenna alignment (see 12-8).

### SAFETY MEASURES TO BE TAKEN WHEN WORKING ON CONTROL AND NAVIGATION SYSTEM

#### 1 - SCOPE

To specify to the personnel the safety measures specific to the control and navigation systems.

#### **CAUTION:**

REFER TO MANUAL 01-0 FOR GENERAL SAFETY MEASURES.

#### 2 - INSTRUCTIONS FOR PROTECTION OF EQUIPMENT

#### A - Flux valve

Never use an ohmmeter to conduct continuity tests on the flux valve windings.

No handling should be made on the flux valve by means of a magnet; however, to perform the SDAP «Heading channel» test program, a special magnet taken from the SDAP tool kit, may be used, to the exclusion of any other.

#### B - Navigation computer

On the ground, energization of the navigation computer should not exceed 15 minutes without ventilation.

#### C - Gyro equipment

Wait at least two minutes after the gyro wheels have stopped before handling the gyro equipment. Failure to observe this time period could result in damage to the gyro equipment.

#### PRINCIPLE OF PITOT-STATIC SYSTEM AND FLIGHT INSTRUMENTS

The pitot-static system senses and distributes the pitot and static pressures to the associated systems:

- the pitot pressure is sensed by measuring the air flow stop pressure on a pitot tube,

- the static pressure is sensed by measuring the local atmospheric pressure through a probe mounted on the nose cone, and through three fuselage static inlets.

The flight instruments supplied by the pitot-static system are as follows:

- standby altimeter (static pressure)
- vertical speed indicator (static pressure)
- Mach/airspeed indicator (static and pitot pressures).

The flight instruments also include:

- accelerometer
- standby horizon
- standby compass
- clock
- angle-of-attack indicator.

An angle-of-attack system sensing the aircraft angle of attack :

- one angle-of-attack probe measuring the local aircraft angle of attack,

- one angle-of-attack indicator transforming the local angle of attack into true angle of attack and displaying it.

The local or true angle of attack (high or low airspeed correction schedule) is distributed to the associated systems by the angle-of-attack probe and the angle-to-attack indicator.

A probe heating circuit is used for deicing:

- pitot pressure probe
- static pressure probe
- angle-of-attack probe.

## STANDBY MAGNETIC COMPASS (figures 3 and 4)

#### 1 - OPERATION

#### A - Principle

A moving assembly carrying a permanent magnet rotates in the local magnetic field.

#### B - Description (see figure 3)

The compass includes:

- mount 1
- compensator unit 2
- bowl 3
- moving assembly 4.

![](_page_64_Figure_13.jpeg)

FIGURE 3 - STANDBY MAGNETIC COMPASS

The compensation devices are as follows:

(1) Lubber line compensation The bowl can turn about the vertical axis when screw 5 is not tightened.

{65}------------------------------------------------

![](_page_65_Picture_0.jpeg)

### MIRAGE F

MANUAL 12

#### (2) Compensator unit (figure 4)

The compensator unit includes:

- a semicircular plate
- an upper assembly
- a lower assembly
- a cover.

Semicircular plate

The semicircular plate is secured to the support plate of the mount. On its upper and lower surfaces, cylindrical recesses are provided for accommodating the two compensator assemblies.

Restricted

Upper compensator assembly

The upper compensator assembly (housed in the upper surface recesses) consists of two gearwheels 5 meshing with each other. One gearwheel meshes with a tangent screw 6 marked NS, which can be actuated from the front.

Each gearwheel accommodates a magnet 4. The relative gearwheel movement creates a magnetic field; the magnetic field direction is parallel with the gearwheel centerline, and its intensity can be varied as required. The gearwheels are held in position in the recesses by a screwed retaining plate 7.

Lower compensator assembly

The lower compensator assembly (housed in the lower surface recesses) is similar to the upper assembly. It is identified by letters EW. Its operation is similar to that of the upper assembly (gearwheels 2 and tangent screw 3) but its action is shifted by 90°.

The cover is installed from the front of the compensator unit; it protects the tangent screw heads.

# MIRAGE F

#### STANDBY HORIZON (Figure 5)

#### 1 - OPERATION

The vertical reference is obtained by using the properties of a gyro with two degrees of freedom, associated with an erection system slaving the gyro axis to the local vertical.

A manual resetting device permits the gyro to be rapidly switched on in a direction close to the vertical, in straight flight or on ground.

The roll and pitch data are displayed to the pilot and distributed by potentiometer transmitters to acquisition unit 50Z.

The monitoring system controlling the warning flag acts on the power supply of the gyro rotor driving motor.

The warning flag is also visible during the manual gyro resetting sequence.

NOTE: The vertical information is available about 50 seconds after energization of the standby horizon.

When the apparent vertical departs from the gyro axis by more than  $9^{\circ}$ , the erection effect is cut off.

{68}------------------------------------------------

![](_page_68_Picture_2.jpeg)

![](_page_68_Figure_3.jpeg)

FIGURE 5 - STANDBY HORIZON

{69}------------------------------------------------

![](_page_69_Picture_0.jpeg)

MIRAGE F
MANUAL 12

#### **ACCELEROMETER**

#### 1 - PRINCIPLE

A moving weight guided along the sensitive axis of the pickoff, moves as the acceleration component along this axis varies.

The movement is limited by the action of a spiral spring, the counteracting torque of which balances the torque resulting from the weight acceleration. A sealed oil damper stabilizes the weight in the balanced position. The weight movement is transmitted to an index through a gear train and a wire-and pulley device.

Two moving indexes display the maximum values reached in each direction. A pushbutton is used for returning the indexes to g=1. They are driven by the main index displaying the acceleration.

#### 2 - PRINCIPLE (Modification FH310 incorporated)

The front panel is in the form of a graduated circular dial in front of which three pointers move:

- -a main pointer continuously indicating the apparent vertical acceleration,
- -two secondary pointers used to record the maximum apparent vertical acceleration values reached in each direction,
  - -a button used to reset the secondary pointers to the main pointer position.

The operating principle is basically the same as the above-described principle. The weight movement is transmitted to the main pointer trhough a gear train.

![](_page_70_Picture_0.jpeg)

### ANGLE-OF-ATTACK SYSTEM (Figures 6, 7 and 8)

#### 1 - SCOPE (Figure 6)

The scope of the angle-of-attack system is to furnish an angle-of-attack information.

The local angle of attack (il) measured by the probe is distributed :

- to the angle-of-attack indicator,
- to main computer (104F) and symbol generator box (35A) which compute :
  - it1 as per the low airspeed schedule,
  - it2 as per the high airspeed schedule
- to the air data computer which makes the angle-of attack corrections required for the radar,
- to the acquisition unit (50Z) and the slat servo unit (193C).

Figure 6 shows the distribution of the associated systems.

#### 2 - OPERATION (Figures 7 and 8)

#### A - Angle-of-attack probe :

#### (1) Principle

The probe comprises:

- a body fixed to the aircraft structure
- a cylindrical tube equipped with vanes. These vanes are housed inside chambers in the body and the tube can rotate in relation to the latter.

The tube protrudes out of the aircraft skin and is subjected to the air stream pressures. Two slots provided along the tube generatrixes, at  $\pm$  45° (when the tube is in position of equilibrium), receive the pressures which are directed toward the body chambers. When these pressures are equal, the vanes are in equilibrium and the tube remains in the direction of the air stream (aerodynamic zero). This position corresponds to the local angle of attack.

#### (2) Operation

The probe starts operating as the aircraft AC power systems are energized. Probe heater switch 2F supplies the deicing resistor of the tube which is temperature-controlled to 95° C.

When rotating, the tube drives:

- 2 synchro-receivers delivering the angle-of-attack information to the angle-of-attack indicator, air data computer, main computer and symbol generator box,
- 2 potentiometers transmitting the angle-of-attack information to acquisition unit 50Z and slat servo unit 193C.

The electrical zero of these components is set to the aerodynamic zero of the tube.

#### (3) Testing

A test motor controlled by switch 74F, moves the probe between -5 and  $+35^{\circ}$  in relation to the FRL (see figure 7).

{71}------------------------------------------------

![](_page_71_Picture_0.jpeg)

MIRAGE F

MANUAL 12

#### B - Angle-of-attack indicator

#### (1) Operation

The local angle of attack is received by synchro-receiver S2.

A servo system repeats the received angle of attack and drives transform :

- the local angle of attack into true angle of attack (low airspeed schedule at M=0.3) for actuating the indicator tape, it1 > 8° contact and it1 > 14° contact.

A threshold detector independent of the repeater channel and connected to the stator of synchroreceiver S2 controls a relay when the it1 angle-of-attack angles exceed 17°. The contact of this relay is used to operate the warning horn as this limit angle-of-attack angle is exceeded.

An it1  $\geqslant$  14° contact controlled by the it1 drive energizes relay 81F which sends a 28 V signal to the engine and a ground signal to warning horn 7W when autopilot 39C is connected.

The warning horn is cut off if the RH shock absorber is compressed.

An it1 > 8° contact controlled by the it1 drive illuminates the green, yellow and red lighted blocks when it1 exceeds 8°.

#### (2) Monitoring

The indicator monitoring system governs:

- 26 V/400 Hz power supply from 26 V AC transformer (26F),

- angle-of-attack repeating by analyzing the error signal of the slaving loop.

Malfunctioning causes extension of the warning flag on the angle-of-attack indicator.

#### C - Test of angle-of-attack system

The angle-of-attack system can be tested by performing:

- external simulation with an angle-of-attack gauge,
- integrated test through switch 74F.

The integrated test permits the probe to be set to  $\pm~20^\circ$  from the average position (mechanical zero). These positions correspond to  $5^\circ$  nose-down and  $35^\circ$  nose-up in relation to the FRL.

(1) Position  $-5^{\circ}$ . A winding of the test motor brings the probe to position  $-5^{\circ}$ .

At the same time, the angle-of-attack indicator reads  $0.35^{\circ}$ .

(2) Position 35°. A second winding of the test motor brings the probe to position 35°. At the same time, the angle-of-attack indicator reads 21.5°.

The information contact corresponding to it  $1>17^\circ$  operates warning horn 7W (or to it  $1>14^\circ$  when the autopilot is engaged).

The microswitch of the RH U/C leg is shorted during the test.

### PITOT-STATIC SYSTEM (figure 15)

The system includes the following components:

- static pressure probe 80F with static pressure inlets S1 and S2
- fuselage static pressure inlets S3, S4 and S5
- pitot pressure probes 6F and 13F
- pitot-static lines
- drain fittings and unit
- flight instruments directly connected to the pitot-static lines :
  - standby altimeter 51F
  - mach/airspeed indicator 55F
  - vertical speed indicator 3.

The purpose of the aircraft pitot-static system is to collect and distribute the total and static pressures.

NOTE: The metal tubing of static lines S1 and S2 provides bonding between the static pressure probe and radome ferrule.

### 1 - PRESSURE PROBES AND INLETS AND PITOT-STATIC LINES (figure 9)

A - Pitot pressure probe T1 and pilot's pitot pressure probe T2.

The pitot pressures from probes T1 and T2 are used by the following equipment:

- (1) Pressure T1: mach/airspeed indicator 55F, slat IAS aneroid 108C and flap IAS aneroid 109C,
- (2) Pressure T2: air data computer 17F, dashpot jack 194C and pressure center 53Z.
- B Static pressure inlets S1 and S2

The static pressures from static pressure inlets S1 and S2 of the probe are fed through the nose cone to a quick-disconnect connector block mounted on frame 1. The connector block directs the pressures to the following equipment:

- (1) Pressure S1: standby altimeter 51F, mach/airspeed indicator 55F, Mach number switch 56F, slat IAS aneroid 108C and flap IAS aneroid 109C.
- (2) Pressure S2: air data computer 17F, autopilot 39C and pressure center 53Z.
- C Static pressure inlets S3, S4 and S5.

The static pressures from fuselage inlets S3, S4 and S5 directly supply the following equipment:

- (1) Pressure S3: vertical speed indicator 3 and cabin pressure regulator 10.
- (2) Pressure S4: dashpot jack 194C.
- (3) Pressure S5: overpressure/underpressure valve 26H.

#### 2 - CONNECTION OF COMPONENTS

| COMPONENT                        | PRES  | SURE        |
|----------------------------------|-------|-------------|
| COMPONENT                        | Pitot | Static      |
| Mach/airspeed indicator          | Т1    | S1          |
| Standby altimeter                |       | <b>S</b> 1  |
| Vertical speed indicator         |       | \$3         |
| Air data computer                | T2    | \$2         |
| Autopilot                        |       | S2          |
| Dashpot jack                     | T2    | S4          |
| Cabin pressure regulator         |       | \$3         |
| Slat IAS aneroid                 | T1    | S1          |
| Flap IAS aneroid                 | T1    | S1          |
| Mach number switch               | T1 .  | S1          |

#### 3 - DRAINING OF LINES

Certain pitot and static pressure lines are provided with drains at their lowest points :

- three drains identified 7 for static line S1, 8 for static line S2 and pitot line T2, 9 for static line S4 and pitot line T2.

The distribution lines of static inlets S3 and S5 and pitot probe T1 are not fitted with drains, as the inlets and the probe are located at the lowest point of the system.

#### 4 - STANDBY ALTIMETER

Static pressure variations distort an aneroid. A distorted aneroid causes the rotation of a shaft which drives the display pointers.

A thermal compensation device (bimetal strip) controls the operation between -20 and +50 °C. The lighting power is supplied through terminals A and B of the receptacle.

#### 5 - MACH/AIRSPEED INDICATOR

The Mach/airspeed indicator includes a pitot-static mechanism and an altimeter mechanism which are independent of each other. Each of them is fitted with an aneroid.

The pitot-static aneroid is subjected to pressure  $\Delta p$ , which is the difference between the pitot pressure (inside the aneroid) and the static pressure (inside the case). When deformed, the aneroid drives a disk pointer in front of a fixed dial graduated in knots, to give the airspeed.

The altimeter aneroid is subjected to the static pressure.

When deformed, the aneroid drives a moving dial graduated in Mach numbers behind a disk pointer which is cut away to permit reading.

{79}------------------------------------------------

![](_page_79_Picture_0.jpeg)

# MIRAGE F

#### 6 - VERTICAL SPEED INDICATOR

Principle:

The vertical speed indicator is composed of :

- one thermostatic capacitor
- one differential pressure gauge.

The aneroid of the differential pressure gauge is subjected:

- inside, to the existing static pressure.
- outside, to the pressure of the thermostatic capacitor (previous static pressure).

The pressure differential measured by the differential pressure gauge represents the vertical component of the aircraft speed at a given moment.

### PROBE HEATING CIRCUITS (Figure 16)

#### 1 - SCOPE

The heating system controls deicing of:

- pilot's pitot probe T1 6F
- pitot probe T2 13F
- angle-of-attack probe 14F
- static pressure probe 80F.

#### 2 - OPERATION

#### A - Power supplies :

When closed, probe heater switch 2F supplies:

- pilot's pitot probe T1 through the energization coil of current relay 5F
- pitot probe T2 through pitot static transformer 10F
- angle-of-attack probe
- static pressure probe through energized relay 78F and energization coil of heater monitoring relay 79F.

#### B - Monitoring:

Energization of relays 5F and 79F causes extinction of the «PITOT» light on the failure warning panel.

Failure of the power supply of pilot's pitot probe T1 or of the static pressure probe causes illumination of the «PITOT» warning light.

Failure of the power supply of pitot probe T2 13F or of the angle-of-attack probe is not signalled.

CONTROLE D'ETANCHEITE DES CIRCUITS ANEMO-METRIQUES

Page 12-1-504

#### ESSAI D'ETANCHEITE DU CIRCUIT STATIQUE S5

Si la tolérance < 5 mb/mn ne peut être respectée, effectuer l'essai d'étanchéité comme suit :

- a) Débrancher la tuyauterie statique de la soupape
- b) Obturer cette tuyauterie, côté avion, (voir NOTA)
- c) Effectuer l'étanchéité du circuit S5 seul :
  - appliquer une dépression de 200 mb dans le circuit (correspondant à une altitude de 6000 pieds)
  - attendre une minute pour la stabilisation du circuit
  - . isoler le circuit de la source de dépression et vérifier que la fuite ne dépasse pas 0,5 mb/mn.
- d) Ramener progressivement le circuit à l'ambiance
- e) Déposer le système d'obturation de la tuyauterie statique
- f) Rebrancher la tuyauterie statique S5 sur la soupape de surpression
- g) Effectuer un essai d'étanchéité cabine (Voir 09-2-502)

NOTA: Matériel d'obturation nécessaire:

- Raccord droit ARSAERO Réf. RA 5606
- Obturateur ARSAERO Réf. R 10406
- Bague de compression ARSAERO Réf. R5006
- Ecrou spécial ARSAERO Réf. R 5306

CE-EE-CK-ED-AD-CJ-EJ-EQ-CH-EH

FILING INSTRUCTIONS: To be added at the beginning of the manual 2 after the "list of modifications included in the manual"

#### PITOT-STATIC SYSTEM LEAK TEST

Page 12-1-504

1/2

#### TEST OF STATIC SYSTEM S5

If the tolerance < 5 mb per minute cannot be maintained accomplish the leak test as follows:

- a) Disconnect the static line from the valve
- b) Cap this line on aircraft side (see NOTE)
- c) Accomplish leak test of the S5 system only:
  - . subject the system to a negative pressure of 200 mb (equivalent of a 6000 ft altitude)
  - wait one minute for the system to stabilize
  - . isolate the system from the negative pressure source and check that the leak does not exceed 0.5 mb per minute
- d) Slowly return the system to ambient pressure
- e) Remove the cap from the static line
- f) Reconnect the S5 static line to the pressure relief valve
- g) Accomplish a cockpit leak test (see 09-2-502)

NOTE: Capping equipment required:

- ARSAERO straight coupling P/N RA 5606
- ARSAERO cap

P/N R 10406

- ARSAERO compression ring P/N R 5006
- ARSAERO special nut P/N R 5306

### PRINCIPLE OF AIR DATA SYSTEM (Figure 1)

The air data system includes:

- one air data computer 17F
- one temperature probe 43F.

The air data computer collects data from:

- total pressure system T2
- static pressure system S2
- temperature probe 43F
- angle-of-attack probe 14F.

From pickoffs sensing static pressure, dynamic pressure, total temperature and the local angle of attack, the air data computer generates :

- static pressure Ps
- air density
- pressure altitude H
- Mach number M
- true airspeed TAS
- dynamic pressure ΔP
- indicated airspeed IAS
- total temperature Tt
- true angle of attack

Figure 1 shows the distribution of the data generated by the system.

MANUAL 12

#### AIR DATA COMPUTER

#### 1 - GENERAL (figure 4)

The air data computer is a unit processing, generating and distributing data in connection with the aircraft flight (airspeed, altitude and angle of attack).

These data result from four physical data:

- static pressure
- collected by the pitot-static system
- total pressure
- total temperature, collected by the total temperature probe
- local angle of attack, measured by the angle-of-attack probe.

The measured static pressure Ps is subjected to a correction for antenna error dPs depending on the Mach number.

 $\Delta P = Pt - Ps$  is computed from the two pressure measurements and also subjected to a correction against the Mach number.

The following data are generated from these corrected measurements:

$$M = f \left[ \begin{pmatrix} \Delta P \\ Ps \end{pmatrix} \right]$$

$$IAS = f(\Delta P)$$

$$TAS = f(M, Tt)$$

#### 2 - PICKOFFS

The following units transform the physical data into electrical information, usable by the computation channels:

- for Ps, one pickoff in the computer,
- for  $\Delta P$ , one pickoff in the computer, also receiving Ps and Pt,
- for Tt, the total temperature probe of the aircraft,
- for local angle of attack (il), the angle-of-attack probe.

The pickoffs deliver a DC voltage which is a linear function of the measured datum.

The temperature probe forms one branch of a resistor bridge. The electrical information corresponding to Tt measurement is generated by the computer from the resistance variation of the probe.

The angle-of-attack probe delivers a synchro signal (XYZ) depending on the local angle of attack.

### 3 - STATIC PRESSURE Ps AND ALTITUDE H CHANNEL (figures 5 and 10)

#### A - Computation of Ps

The output voltage from the Ps pickoff depends on the static pressure collected by the inlet of the pitot-static system.

This pressure is erroneous because of various sources, and specially that of the Mach number. Computing the actual Ps consists in correcting according to a Mach number schedule, the raw Ps for antenna errors dPs which are specific to the aircraft and the pitot-static system. The  $\epsilon$ Ps voltage resulting from Ps processing is used to generate two voltages (V1 and V2) intended to supply the slave control potentiometer. The servo-driven slider of this potentiometer delivers an error voltage  $\epsilon Ps$  which supplies the electronic control circuit of servomotor B1.

{105}------------------------------------------------

![](_page_105_Picture_0.jpeg)

{106}------------------------------------------------

![](_page_106_Figure_0.jpeg)

{107}------------------------------------------------

MANUAL 12

![](_page_107_Picture_2.jpeg)

Motor B1 drives:

- the following repeater potentiometers :
  - H2, H3, H4, H5, H6
  - H fine 2
  - air density
  - Ps1, Ps2
- the servo system control potentiometer
- synchro-transmitters BCX1 and BCX2
- the altitude coder
- contact track AT H allowing channel testing to be controlled with a simulation of Ps = 227.3 mb corresponding to a computer H value of 11,000 meters.

Voltage sPs is also used to supply the electronic circuits controlling the relays associated with H > 8,400 m and H > 12,000 m contacts; voltage  $\epsilon$ Ps is fed to the H monitoring circuit (monitor).

#### 4 - DYNAMIC PRESSURE AND IAS CHANNEL (Figures 6 and 10)

#### A - Computation of $\Delta P$

The output voltage from the  $\Delta P$  pickoff depends on the raw dynamic pressure generated from data Pt and Ps collected by the pitot static system. This voltage is corrected for antenna error  $d\Delta P$  versus data M and Ps. Resulting information  $a\Delta P$  is used for computing the IAS and Mach number information.

#### B — Computation of IAS

Voltage  $a\Delta P$  is used to supply one end of resistor bridge R1-R2, the other end being supplied by the wiper of potentiometer  $\Delta Pn$  driver by the IAS servo system. Error signal  $\epsilon\Delta P$ , collected at the common point of R1-R2, supplies the electronic circuit controlling servo motor B2.

Motor B2 drives:

- repeater potentiometers IAS1 and IAS2,
- servo system control potentiometer ΔPn
- contact track AT IAS allowing channel testing to be controlled with a simulation of  $\Delta P = 426.8$  mb corresponding to a computed M value of 1.35 and to a computed IAS value of 890 km/h.

Voltage  $a\Delta P$  is also used to supply the electronic circuits controlling the relays associated with the various IAS contacts; voltage  $\epsilon\Delta P$  is fed to the IAS monitoring circuit (monitor).

#### 5 - MACH NUMBER CHANNEL (Figures 6 and 10)

The Mach number (M) is a function of  $\frac{\Delta P}{Ps}$  generated from the voltages corrected for antenna error, i.e. aPs (H channel) and  $a\Delta P$  ( $\Delta P$  channel).

Voltage aPs is inverted to obtain voltage cPs to which is added voltage  $d\Delta P$  available after IAS computation; this voltage is used to supply one end of the M servo system control potentiometer.

The other end of that potentiometer is fed with voltage  $a\Delta P$ , available after  $\Delta P$  computation, and the wiper, driven by the M servo system, supplies error signal  $\epsilon M$  used to supply the electronic circuit controlling servo motor B3.

1

![](_page_107_Picture_28.jpeg)

Restricted

2-109

{108}------------------------------------------------

A 8 

{109}------------------------------------------------

![](_page_109_Picture_2.jpeg)

Motor B3 drives :

- the synchro-transmitter
- repeater potentiometers M1 and M2
- the potentiometer intended for TAS computation
- the potentiometer used for antenna correction computations (dps)
- potentiometers Mit1 and Mit2 used for true angle-of-attack computation
- the servo system control potentiometer
- contact track AT M allowing channel testing with a computed M value of 1.35.

Voltages  $a\Delta P$  and aPs are also used to supply the electronic circuits controlling the relays associated with the various Mach number contacts; voltage  $\epsilon M$  is fed to the M monitoring circuit (monitor).

#### 6 - TOTAL TEMPERATURE CHANNEL (Figures 7 and 10)

The total temperature results from the measurement of a voltage across the terminals of a resistor bridge consisting of fixed elements internal to the computer, and of an element varying as the temperature and which forms the Tt probe. The Tt probe is connected to the computer through a three-conductor circuit used to correct for the spurious resistance due to the probe-to-computer connection. The Tt information is generated in the form of  $\sqrt{Tt}$  and is used to supply the TAS channel control potentiometer and the electronic circuit controlling the relay which controls «Limit» warning light 11W through the Tt  $\geq$  135 °C contact (see manual 08-3).

#### 7 - TRUE AIRSPEED CHANNEL (Figures 7 and 10)

True airspeed TAS is generated from the  $\sqrt{\text{Tt}}$  data as computed above and from M, which is computed by the Mach number channel.

The TAS = f (M $\sqrt{\text{Tt}}$ ) function is performed by a potentiometer supplied with voltage  $\sqrt{\text{Tt}}$  and whose wiper is driven by the Mach number servo system. The signal collected by the potentiometer wiper is summed up with the TAS signal fed by the TAS potentiometer wiper and supplies the electronic circuit controlling servo motor B4.

#### Motor B4 drives:

- repeater potentiometers TAS1, TAS2, TAS3, TAS4 and TAS5
- the servo system control potentiometer
- contact track AT TAS allowing channel testing with a simulation of Tt = 22.7 °C corresponding to a computed TAS value of 771.6 kt.

#### 8 - TRUE ANGLE-OF-ATTACK CHANNEL (Figures 8 and 10)

The true angle of attack (it) is generated from the local angle of attack (it) provided by the angle-of-attack probe and including corrections io and K which vary as the Mach number.

The io (M) and K (M) functions are performed by potentiometers Mit1 and Mit2 whose wipers are driven by the Mach number channel servo system.

Synchro BCR1 receives at its rotor the il signal from the angle-of-attack probe. The rotor is driven by the it servo system. As slaving proceeds, the it servo system control potentiometer wiper gives the repeated angle-of-attack value. The signal from BCR1 is therefore the il signal offset by the repeated angle-of-attack value. This signal supplies one end of resistor bridge R3-R4 through the NC contact of the test relay. The other end of the bridge is supplied with the sum of the correction signals from Mit1 and Mit2 potentiometer wipers and with the repeated angle-of-attack.

### POWER SUPPLIES (Figure 9)

The aircraft power system supplies the air data computer with:

- 115 V/400 Hz, phase A through AC system 1,
- filtered 26 V/400 Hz through 26 V AC transformer 126F.

The air data computer generates the following voltages from the 115 V/400 Hz supply :

- + and 5 V AC reference voltage for the monitoring devices
- + and 6 V rectified voltage for the servo motor control push-pull units
- + and 10 V reference voltage for the computing circuits
- + 15 V, 15 V and 15 V ground for the pressure pickoffs and amplifiers
- + 25 V and 25 V ground for the power supply of relays.

The 26 V/400 Hz voltage is used for the power supply of altitude synchro-transmitter BCX2.

#### MONITORING AND TESTING

#### 1 - MONITORING (figure 10)

#### A - Principle

The servo systems repeating data H, IAS, M, TAS and it from the air data computer are monitored.

Each monitor analyses the error voltage of the monitored servo system and delivers a failure information when the error voltage exceeds a preset threshold for a period of time  $\Delta t$  (approx. 2 seconds for H, IAS, M and TAS channels, and approx. 0.3 second for it channel).

The failure of a servo system triggers:

- a master alarm
- an individual alarm corresponding to the monitored datum.

#### B - Operation

#### (1) Air data computer master alarm

The detection by a monitor of a failure affecting a servo system repeating H, IAS, M, TAS and it causes deenergization of relay K7 which then causes:

- the «ADC» light on failure warning panel 1W to come on
- the reliability signal intended for autopilot 39C to be cut off.
- (2) Individual alarms
- (a) Altitude coder output (figure symbol: 35)

The detection of a failure in the servo system repeating H causes deenergization of relays K1 and K2.

Relay K1 cuts off the coder common circuit

#### (b) Symbol generator box 35A

The detection of a failure in the servo systems repeating H, IAS, M and TAS causes deenergization of relays K1, K2, K3, K4 and K5 respectively.

Relays K1, K3, K4 and K5 cause the corresponding reliability signals intended for symbol generator box 35A to be cut off.

#### (c) Main computer 104F

Relays K2 and K5, deenergized by a failure in the servo systems repeating H and TAS respectively, cause the reliability signals intended for the main computer to be cut off.

#### (d) Roll/pitch output multiplier 38F

The detection of a failure in the servo systems repeating it or M causes deenergization of relay K6 which, in turn, causes the it reliability signal intended for the roll/pitch output multiplier to be cut off.

{119}------------------------------------------------

#### MANUAL 12

![](_page_119_Picture_2.jpeg)

Restricted

#### (f) Flying aid amplifier 48C

The detection of a failure affecting the servo systems repeating H or M causes deenergization of relay K8 which, in turn, causes the H + M reliability signal intended for flying aid amplifier 48C to be cut off.

#### 2 - SELF-TEST (Figure 11)

The operation of the H, IAS, M, TAS and it servo systems is tested by switching the inputs of the Ps,  $\Delta P$ , Tt and il channels to reference voltages.

Depressing the test button causes relay K4 to be energized; this relay causes:

- the Ps,  $\Delta P$  and Tt channels to be electronically switched to simulation voltages,
- transistor Q1 to conduct, allowing relay K1 to be energized and to switch the it channel to the test voltage.

From the input simulations tabulated below, the computing channels determine the computed values also tabulated below:

| Simulated values                                                                   | Computed values                                              |
|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------|
| $P_{S} = 227 \text{ mb}$<br>$\Delta P = 427 \text{ mb}$<br>$T_{t} = 22.7^{\circ}C$ | H = 1,000 m<br>M = 1.35<br>TAS = 1430 km/h<br>IAS = 890 km/h |
| U ref.                                                                             | $it = +10^{\circ}$                                           |

The H, IAS, M, TAS and it computing channels each control the opening of the corresponding AT contact when the repeating servo system is correctly positioned. Contacts AT, which are connected in parallel, allow transistor Q2 to conduct and therefore relay K3 to be energized and the test light to come, only when they are all open.

The simulated Mach number can be read from shock-cone position indicator 38C when hydraulic system 1 is under pressure and when the shock-cones are in automatic operating mode.

The simulated altitude can be read from the head-up display (see brief check).

#### 3 - EXTERNAL TESTS (Figure 11)

The above self-test can be electrically controlled:

- by the main computer during navigation system testing,
- from connector J3 located on the front face of the air data computer. For this type of test, the result provided through the closing of one contact in relay K3 is available at test connector J3.

The Ps and  $\Delta P$  pressures and the total temperature can be simulated from test connector J5 located on the front face of the air data computer.

#### TROUBLE SHOOTING IN AIR DATA SYSTEM

#### 1 - SCOPE

This operation is intended to locate a failed component, using the aircraft equipment.

For any symptom reported by the pilot, the mechanic shall confirm the trouble (brief check) by attempting to restore flight conditions:

A — If the symptoms reported by the pilot cannot be reproduced, use the SDAP (See 10-1) complying with the programme specified in the following charts.

B — If the symptoms reported by the pilot are reproduced, follow the procedure described in the following charts.

#### 2 - TROUBLE SHOOTING PROCEDURE

The trouble shooting procedure is described in the following charts.

NOTE: In the case where the SDAP is to be used for detecting a trouble, the applicable programme number is specified in the charts (See 10-1 for preparation and operating procedure).

### PRINCIPLE OF ATTITUDE AND HEADING SYSTEM (Figure 1)

The attitude and heading system, which is incorporated in the DICS (Doppler radar/Inertial unit Combined System) dealt with in 12-6, supplies this system and several other associated systems with vertical reference (roll and pitch) and heading information.

The system includes a vertical reference channel and two heading channels (normal and emergency heading channels).

The vertical reference channel is composed of the following:

- one sensing component : gyro/accelerometer platform 35F (vertical reference gyro)
- one interface electronics box 145F
- one roll/pitch output multiplier 38F
- display components : spherical indicator 28F, head-up display 34A.

The aircraft attitude, sensed by the gyro/accelerometer platform in analog form, is distributed in this form and in the following manner to display components and associated systems :

- directly by the platform to spherical indicator 28F and autopilot 39C
- through the interface electronics box to symbol generator box 35A for head-up display 34A
- through the interface electronics box and the roll/pitch output multiplier to the radar.

The aircraft attitude is also distributed in digital form by the interface electronics box to main computer 104F.

The normal heading channel is composed of the following:

- two sensing components : gyro/accelerometer platform 35F (directional gyro) and flux valve
  - one generating component: interface electronics box 145F
  - display components: spherical indicator 28F, navigation indicator 68F, head-up display 34A.

The interface electronics box receives, in analog form, a directional heading from the gyro/accelerometer platform, and a magnetic heading from the flux valve. With these two data, the interface electronics box generates a gyromagnetic heading (GMH) which it distributes in analog form to the various display components and to all associated systems. The gyromagnetic heading is also distributed in digital form to main computer 104F.

The emergency heading channel is composed of the following:

- one sensing component: flux valve 75F
- one sensing and generating component: emergency gyromagnetic compass 30F
- one transmitting component : interface electronics box 145F
- display components: spherical indicator 28F, navigation indicator 68F, head-up display 34A.

The flux valve monitors the emergency gyromagnetic compass which generates an emergency gyromagnetic heading (EGMH) in analog form which is distributed to all associated systems by the interface electronics box (including main computer 104F).

Figure 1 shows the distribution of roll, pitch and heading data to the various associated systems.

| 145F | Contains most of the electronic components ensuring a correct operation of the gyro/accelerometer platform.  Generates all power supply voltages required for platform operation and provides time delays for platform starting.  Converts and codes the signals fed by platform accelerometers and transmits these signals to the main computer through the digibus link.  In normal operation (Doppler radar/Inertial unit operation), receives the vertical reference correction coded signals fed by the main computer, decodes and converts these signals into control voltages which it transmits to the torque motors of the platform vertical reference gyro.  Transmits the vertical reference data (roll and pitch) in analog form to all associated systems, except the spherical indicator which receives them directly from the platform; these data are transmitted in digital form to the main computer through the digibus link.  Generates the gyromagnetic heading from the directional heading fed by the platform, and from the magnetic heading fed by flux valve 41F.  Distributes the gyromagnetic heading in analog form to all associated systems, including the main computer which also receives this data in digital form through the digibus link.  Monitors all the above data and delivers reliability signals to the various associated systems. | Equipment bay 13-00      |    |    |

### VERTICAL REFERENCE CHANNEL (Figures 4 and 5)

The vertical reference channel is composed of the following:

- gyro/accelerometer platform 35F

- interface electronics box 145F

- spherical indicator 28F

- roll/pitch output multiplier 38F.

#### 1 - OPERATION OF GYRO/ACCELEROMETER CENTER

The center includes two paired components: the platform containing the gyroscopic components and the accelerometers, and the interface electronics box containing the platform electronic servo components, the DICS (Doppler radar/Inertial unit Combined System) connecting components and the power supply.

#### A - Principle

The platform consists of a vertical reference gyro with the gyro rotor axis slaved to the Earth's vertical reference, and two gimbals arranged as follows:

- an inner gimbal (pitch gimbal) supporting the vertical reference gyro and turning about the transverse aircraft axis.

- an outer gimbal (roll gimbal) supporting the pitch gimbal and pivoting in relation to the aircraft structure, about the longitudinal aircraft axis.

Both gimbals are slaved to the vertical axis of the vertical reference gyro by two servo systems, from deviation information collected by two pickups mounted on the longitudinal and transverse axes of the aircraft. The vertical deviation detected by differential transformer PU (Pick Up) is cancelled by motors M101 (roll) and M201 (pitch).

The rotational axis of the gyro rotor is slaved to the local Earth's vertical reference by two mercury levelling switches mounted on the gyro frame along the transverse and longitudinal axes of the aircraft (fast erection during initial setting in emergency erection or alignment performed by main computer in normal operation).

The signals delivered either by the mercury levelling switches or by the main computer are amplified and applied to the precession torque motors which, by gyrostatic effect, permanently return the gyro rotor axis to the Earth's vertical reference.

The combined actions of the servo system slaving the gyro rotor to the Earth's vertical reference and the servo system slaving the roll and pitch gimbals to the vertical reference gyro, result in determining a perfectly horizontal plane.

The aircraft roll and pitch data are delivered by synchro-machines with stators integral with the platform structure and rotors coupled to the gimbal shafts.

#### B - Operation

#### (1) Power supply (figure 8)

The attitude and heading system is energized from the mode and heading selector panel (rotary selector switch set to any position other than «OFF» or «ST.BY») which delivers a 28 V DC voltage controlling relay K801. Relay K802 is energized as the 26 V/400 Hz AC voltage from transformer 126F is applied to relay K801.

All the voltages required for the platform/interface electronics box assembly are supplied by the power supply unit.

#### (2) Starting

The starting sequence is divided into three periods of time ranging between 0 and 100  $\pm$  20 seconds.

#### (a) From 0 to 30 seconds

This period covers the following functions:

- spinning-up of the vertical reference and directional gyro rotors,

- caging of the vertical reference gyro rotor by supplying electro-magnet E1,

- switching of erection circuits to fast erection, but there is no erection since the rotor is caged

{148}------------------------------------------------

at o

{149}------------------------------------------------

# AVIONS MARCEL DASSAULT BREGUET AVIATION

#### Restricted

![](_page_149_Picture_2.jpeg)

- alignment of the roll and pitch gimbals with the corresponding aircraft axes by electronic switching of the gimbal servo systems (amplifiers and motors M101 and M201) onto X and Y deviations as detected by roll and pitch synchro transmitters, respectively CX102 and CX202.

#### (b) From 0 to 45 seconds

This period is characterized by the disabling of the directional gyro elevation gimbal servo system.

#### (c) From 30 to 100 seconds

This period covers the following functions:

- uncaging of vertical reference gyro,
- fast erection of vertical reference gyro : one of the two windings of each torque motor (M1002 and M1003) is supplied with the signal fed by the mercury levelling switches (amplified current),
  - supply of PU's with 6 V/5 kHz,
- electronic switching of roll and pitch gimbal servo systems onto information delivered by pickup PU001,
  - restoring of directional gyro elevation gimbal slaving (after 45 seconds).

#### (d) After 100 seconds: period of normal operation.

The sequence of above-mentioned operations is controlled by time delay circuits (30 sec., 45 sec. and 100 sec.) and by a signal allowing the effects of the watch-dog» and winertial unit reliability signals to be inhibited during the starting phase.

#### (3) Normal operation

Precession commands are generated by the main computer which transmits them to the interface electronics box through the digibus link. These signals are shaped by the «precession control» coupler of interface electronics box 145F to supply one of the two windings of each torque motor M1002 and M1003.

#### (4) Emergency operation

In this mode of operation, one of the two windings of each torque motor M1002 and M1003 is directly supplied with the signal fed by the mercury levelling switches.

The gyro/accelerometer center operates in «emergency erection» mode when :

- a control signal is fed by the main computer (depending on the mode in which the computer generates the basic parameters)
- faulty operation is detected by the software of the interface electronics box (absence of the «Iner-

tial unit reliability» or «watch-dog» bit)

- a control signal is fed by the pilot: «EMG» position selected on mode and heading selector panel.

A special logic controls the illumination of the «AHRS» light on navigation control panel 9F (see 12-6). Warning is provided when the «NAV» position is selected on the mode and heading selector panel and when the gyro/accelerometer center operates in «emergency erection» mode.

{150}------------------------------------------------

MANUAL 12

#### Restricted

#### C -- Conditioning (figure 3P)

To obtain maximum efficiency, the gyro/accelerometer platform is maintained in operation at an even and constant internal temperature of 50  $\pm~2^\circ$  C.

This temperature is obtained by means of fan M001, which agitates the heat emitted by resistors R601, and is regulated by thermistor TH601 controlling relay K301 via a comparator (cut off with T  $\geq$  50° C, energized with T  $\leq$  50° C). With T  $\geq$  50° C and relay K301 at rest, power supplies to R601 and M001 are cut off; however to prevent internal temperature from exceeding a certain value, contact CT003 restores the supply of M001 when this value exceeds 70° C.

A safety device, contact CT601, shorts the power supply to relay K301 when the internal temperature exceeds 85° C (fuse F1 blown).

Temperature builds up in the gyro/accelerometer platform as soon as the rotary selector switch is set to «ST.BY» on the mode and heading selector panel.

![](_page_150_Figure_8.jpeg)

FIGURE 3P - CONDITIONING

{151}------------------------------------------------

MIRAGE F

![](_page_151_Picture_2.jpeg)

D - Monitoring

- (1) The hardware-monitored functions are as follows:
- (a) Roll and pitch gimbal servo systems.
- (b) Pickup power supply voltage: 6 V/6kHz
- (c) Gyro rotor speed
- (d) Interface electronics box power supply.
- (e) Time delay: any time delay inadvertently triggered during normal operation will cause the «time delay reliability» signal to disappear. On the other hand, the «time delay reliability» signal is not applied during the starting phase.

When no anomaly is detected by all the monitoring circuits, the «vertical reference reliability» signal is generated.

- (2) The software-monitored functions are as follows:
- (a) Watch-dog: this is a releasable monostable which should be cyclically reset by the processor program. This system allows the program of the interface electronics box to be checked for correct operation. The «watch-dog» signal is then generated.
- (b) Inertial unit reliability. The «inertial unit reliability» signal is a logical AND between :
  - various results of tests performed by the software of the interface electronics box :
    - coupling to the digibus of the interface electronics box
    - operation of microprocessor and associated components of the interface electronics box
    - synchro/digital conversion
    - reliability of data fed by accelerometers
    - reliability of precession control signals
  - the «vertical reference reliability» signal
  - the emergency erection control signal fed by the main computer.

#### 2 - OPERATION OF ROLL/PITCH OUTPUT MULTIPLIER

#### A - Operation

Upon energization (rotary selector switch of mode and heading selector panel 37F set to a position other than «OFF» or «ST.BY»), the output multiplier is supplied. The roll and pitch repeating servo systems set the output potentiometer wipers and synchro-machines to the roll and pitch references of the platform.

#### B - Monitoring

The roll and pitch repeating servo systems and the power supplies (26 V AC, 115 V AC and 28 V DC) are monitored.

When the roll/pitch output multiplier operates correctly, its transmits the «vertical reference reliability» signal from the interface electronics box to test connector 40Z and to the radar contour mapping system which is also fed with a  $\pm$  28 V voltage from the aircraft system (see 14-2).

{152}------------------------------------------------

\*\*

{153}------------------------------------------------

MIRAGE F

#### 3 - OPERATION OF SPHERICAL INDICATOR

#### A - Principle

The sphere, which is independently subjected to the three heading, pitch and roll data, consists of two hemispheres carried by a disc simulating the equator.

It moves in the heading direction about an axis passing through the center of the disc and through the poles.

This pitch-slaved disc is supported by a roll-slaved stirrup.

#### B - Operation

Each rotating shaft of the sphere is driven by a specific servo channel which is energized:

for heading: by the gyromagnetic heading information controlling torque motor M3 and fed, in normal operation, by the interface electronics box (digital/synchro converter) and, in emergency operation, by the emergency gyromagnetic compass (synchro-transmitter CX1) through the interface electronics box (Fig. 6)

- for roll : by the information fed by platform synchro-transmitter CX101 and controlling torque

motor M1 (Fig. 4),

- for pitch : by the information fed by platform synchro-transmitter CX201 and controlling torque motor M2 (Fig. 5).

The spherical indicator is energized when aircraft electrical systems are energized and when the rotary selector switch of the mode and heading selector panel is set to any position other than «OFF» or «STB».

#### C - Pole setting (figures 4, 5, 6)

When the «N-P» control knob is held in the «P» position, relays RL1 (fig. 4 and 5) and RL2 (figure 6) are energized, switching off the servo-systems of the sphere about its three axes.

In this configuration, the energized relay RL1 applies a 12 V voltage to the pitch servo motor which controls the sphere's rotation about the pitch axis toward the pole (South pole) up to abutment.

#### D - Monitoring (figures 5, 6, 9)

The spherical indicator monitoring system monitors the three servo systems of the sphere.

When a failure is detected in the vertical reference servo systems, the monitoring circuit no longer delivers the signal controlling relay RL5 (Fig. 5). This causes the LF vertical reference reliability signal fed by the interface electronics box and intended for the indicator alarm flag, and the external + 28 V signal intended for the energized contact of heading reliability relay RL6 (Fig. 6) and for test connector 42Z (Fig. 9) to be cut off.

When a failure is detected in the heading servo system, the monitoring circuit no longer delivers the signal controlling relay RL6 (Fig. 6), which causes the external + 28 V signal intended for test connector 42Z to be cut off

#### E — Test operation (Figure 5)

NOTE: Test of the spherical indicator is possible only if:

- all the indicator power supplies are operational,

the reliability signal fed by the interface electronics box energizes terminal K of the indicator.

Depressing the control knob in «N» position causes a fixed voltage to be applied to the three servo systems which then offset the sphere by approx. 15° about the three axes.

MIRAGE F

### NORMAL HEADING CHANNEL (Figure 6)

The normal heading channel is composed of the following:

- gyro accelerometer platform 35F,
- flux valve 41F,
- interface electronics box 145F,
- mode and heading selector panel 37F.
- spherical indicator 28F.

#### 1 - OPERATION

#### A - Flux valve

The flux valve delivers a magnetic heading reference in the form of 800 Hz electrical signals to the interface electronics box.

#### B - Directional gyro (figure 6)

The directional gyro consists of a rotor with a horizontal shaft, and three gimbals arranged as follows:

- one elevation gimbal with horizontal shaft, in which turns the gyro rotor shaft (the elevation gimbal and gyro rotor shafts form an angle of  $90^{\circ}$ ),
- one heading gimbal with vertical shaft ZZ', accommodating the elevation gimbal and gyro rotor assembly,
- one pitch gimbal slaved to the vertical reference gyro, supporting all the above components, and mounted horizontal inside the roll gimbal of the platform.

As the gyro rotor and elevation gimbal assembly must be permanently kept horizontal to obtain correct heading data, two servo systems are required:

(1) Heading gimbal shaft slaving to vertical reference

This is obtained with the pitch gimbal slaved to the vertical reference gyro.

(2) Elevation gimbal slaving to heading gimbal perpendicular component

This is obtained with the following:

- one optical pickup detecting the elevation gimbal position relative to the heading gimbal PU002, (deviation and direction detection),
  - one deviation signal amplifier,
- one torque motor MC301, with the stator secured to the pitch gimbal and the rotor linked to vertical shaft ZZ' of the heading gimbal to cause the gyro rotor to precess in the suitable direction so as to return the elevation gimbal perpendicular to the heading gimbal.

Synchro-transmitter CX001 driven by heading gimbal shaft ZZ' provides the directional heading to the interface electronics box.

{158}------------------------------------------------

\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*\*

{159}------------------------------------------------

MANUAL 12

![](_page_159_Picture_3.jpeg)

#### C - Interface electronics box

#### (1) Operation in normal gyromagnetic heading mode

Platform synchro-transmitter CX001 delivers a directional heading to the interface electronics box. This heading is received by the synchro/digital coding unit.

The magnetic heading from the flux valve is also received by the synchro-digital coding unit of the interface electronics box through the removable compensator.

From these two data, the software of the interface electronics box generates a gyromagnetic heading (magnetic heading filtered by directional heading) and delivers this heading in digital form :

- to the main computer through the digibus link and through required couplers,

- to a digital/synchro converter which converts the digital information into an analog information.

At the output of the digital/synchro converter, the gyromagnetic heading is distributed to the associated systems through the NO contact of relays K1 and K2 controlled either by the interface electronics box monitoring circuits or by the «EMG» position of the rotary selector switch of the mode and heading selector panel.

#### (2) Operation in emergency gyromagnetic heading mode

This operating mode is controlled:

- either automatically by the monitoring system of the interface electronics box : absence of «magnetic monitoring reliability» or «watch-dog» signal,

or manually by setting the rotary selector switch of the mode and heading selector panel to «EMG».

In this operating mode, relays K1 and K2 switch the analog outputs intended for all associated systems to the emergency gyromagnetic heading data delivered by emergency gyromagnetic compass 30F.

A special logic generates a signal controlling the illumination of the «CG90» light on navigation control unit 9F (see 12-6) when the system operates in emergency heading mode with the rotary selector switch of the mode and heading selector unit set to «NAV» or «EMG».

#### (3) Starting

The use of gyromagnetic heading data delivered by the interface electronics box is possible immediately after the time required for platform starting has elapsed.

The effect of the «magnetic monitoring reliability» and «watch-dog» signals is inhibited throughout the starting phase.

#### D - Monitoring

The «magnetic monitoring reliability» and «watch-dog» are generated by a monitoring program of the interface electronics box.

- (1) Watch-dog: see vertical reference channel.
- (2) Magnetic monitoring reliability. This signal is generated if the following conditions exist:
- (a) No anomaly is detected in the following monitored functions:
  - synchro amplifier
  - magnetic monitoring (probability check)
  - digital/synchro and synchro/digital coding
  - digibus coupling.

### MIRAGE F

MANUAL12

- microprocessor and microprocessor components associated with the interface electronics box.

(b) The vertical reference reliability signal (see vertical reference channel) is absent.

#### E - Flux valve compensation

The compensation of flux valve 41F consists in lubber line correction, semi-circular correction and quadrantal correction.

#### (1) Lubber line correction

This correction is performed by positioning the flux valve on its support.

#### (2) Semi-circular and quadrantal corrections

These corrections are performed from «SEMI-CIRCULAR» «N/S-E/W» and «QUADRANTAL» controls located underneath the removable cover at the upper part of interface electronics box 145F.

These controls drive the potentiometers delivering corrective voltages applicable to the data delivered by the flux valve.
