### EMERGENCY HEADING CHANNEL (Figure 7)

The emergency heading channel is composed of the following:

- emergency gyromagnetic compass 30F,
- mode and heading selector panel 37F,
- flux valve 75F.
- interface electronics box 145F.

#### 1 - OPERATION OF EMERGENCY HEADING CHANNEL IN STABILIZED FLIGHT

#### A - Flux valve

The flux valve supplies the emergency gyromagnetic compass with a magnetic heading reference in the form of 800 Hz electrical signals.

#### B - Emergency gyromagnetic compass

This is a gyro with two degrees of freedom in elevation and heading.

The gyro is composed of three major sections:

- one rotor,
- one elevation gimbal,
- one heading gimbal.

The gyro rotor shaft turns in the elevation gimbal and the gyro rotor is driven at 24,000 rpm by a synchronous motor. The rotational axis keeps a fixed horizontal direction.

The elevation gimbal remains perpendicular to the heading gimbal.

The heading gimbal is slaved to the magnetic heading information. It is free with reference to the case integral with the airframe along shaft ZZ'.

Torque motors MC1 and MC2 slaving the elevation and heading gimbals are mounted:

MC1: on elevation shaft YY' for slaving the heading gimbal MC2: on heading shaft ZZ' for slaving the elevation gimbal.

#### Elevation gimbal slaving

The purpose of elevation slaving is to keep the heading and elevation gimbals perpendicular to each other. The perpendicular position is monitored by an optical electronic system which detects any deviation and delivers a 0 or 180° phase signal depending on whether the deviation is positive or negative. This signal, applied to control phase of torque motor MC2, generates a torque on the heading gimbal which causes the rotor to precess in the appropriate direction so as to bring the elevation gimbal back to 90° from the heading gimbal.

#### (2) Heading gimbal slaving

The purpose of heading gimbal slaving is to keep the shaft XX' of the gyro rotor strictly parallel to the axis of the magnetic North detected by the flux valve. Synchro detector CT1 detects the parallelism variation between these two axes and supplies an error signal which, amplified, is used to supply the control phase of torque motor MC1. The torque applied by MC1 to elevation gimbal shaft YY', causes the rotor to precess in such a way as to bring back its XX' shaft parallel to the axis of the magnetic North.

{165}------------------------------------------------

![](_page_165_Picture_0.jpeg)

# MIRAGE F

#### 2 - OPERATION OF EMERGENCY HEADING CHANNEL ON STARTING

#### A - Starting sequence

On energization («OFF-ON» switch of the mode and heading selector panel to «ON»), the gyro is in any position. The starting sequence is as follows:

- on energization, the gyro rotor is supplied and starts spinning up,

- after 10 seconds and up to a time shorter than 1 minute (depending on the initial deviation between the heading of the gyro rotor axis and the magnetic heading), fast resetting of the elevation and azimuth gimbals takes place automatically. The heading resetting rate is then positively higher than  $70^{\circ}$ /min., since the gyro rotor has not yet reached its nominal speed. Fast resetting is automatically stopped when the heading deviation becomes lower than 2.5 to  $5^{\circ}$ .

- the remaining heading deviation is suppressed in slow resetting mode (normal operation). The

gyro rotor reaches the nominal speed 80 seconds after switching-on.

#### B - Slow resetting/fast resetting selection

Slow or fast resetting operation is determined by the resetting logic.

This logic receives the following data:

- end of 10 sec. time delay

- MH - EGMH heading deviation

- manual fast resetting control.

#### It acts on:

- relay K1

- latitude correction (potentiometer P1).

The energization of relay K1 shorts resistor R1 and potentiometer P1, which causes the following:

- for elevation resetting, it increases the voltage applied to the energization winding of torque motor MC2 to increase its speed;

for heading resetting, it increases the voltage applied to both the energization winding and the control winding of torque motor MC1 by modifying the slaving amplifier gain, to increase its speed.

Latitude correction is cut off in fast resetting mode by suppressing power supply to potentiome-

A monitoring unit causes relay K2 to be energized as soon as the servo systems operate in slow resetting mode. Relay K2 delivers a navigation indicator reliability signal to the main computer and the interface electronics box.

Fast resetting can be controlled manually on the ground or in flight through the fast resetting

selector of the mode and heading selector panel.

#### NOTE:

(1) It is possible to continue fast resetting up to  $0^{\circ}$ , when the heading deviation is less than 2.5 to  $5^{\circ}$ , provided the selector switch is kept on «ERECT».

(2) When the MH - EGMH deviation is between 175 and 185°, fast resetting does not occur Every time the emergency heading channel is started, the selector switch must be maintained at «ERECT» to preclude this error.

#### 3 - FLUX VALVE COMPENSATION - LATITUDE CORRECTION

#### A - Emergency heading channel compensation.

Flux valve magnetic compensation is performed on emergency gyromagnetic compass 30F using two N-S and E-W potentiometers which apply correcting DC voltages to the windings of the flux valve.

#### B - Latitude correction

Latitude correction is performed by injecting a voltage into the input of the heading servo amplifier through potentiometer P1.

Its purpose is to correct the gyro drift according to the latitude by applying a constant angular variation to the directional heading.

This voltage corresponding to the angular variation is a mean value for the latitude in which the aircraft is to be operating during the mission.

### MONITORING OF ATTITUDE AND HEADING SYSTEM (Figure 9)

Attitude and heading system monitoring is performed by the following:

- interface electronics box 145F
- emergency gyromagnetic compass 30F
- roll/pitch output multiplier 38F
- spherical indicator 28F.

#### 1 - INTERFACE ELECTRONICS BOX

The monitoring hardware and software system of the interface electronics box generates three reliability discrete signals.

#### A - Vertical reference reliability

This signal is directly fed by the monitoring system associated with gyro/accelerometer center ancillary systems (see vertical reference channel) and is independent of the operating mode (normal or emergency erection) of the center.

This signal is transmitted to the following:

- symbol generator box 35A
- spherical indicator 28F
- test connector 40Z
- test connector 137Z if no anomaly is detected by the internal monitoring system of the roll/pitch output multiplier.

#### B - Heading reliability

This discrete signal indicates that the gyromagnetic heading transmitted to the various equipment items is correctly generated. It is applied if:

- the system does not operate in «emergency heading» mode (see normal heading channel),
- no anomaly is detected by the internal monitoring system of the emergency gyromagnetic compass (CG90) reliability) when the system operates in «emergency heading» mode.

This reliability signal is transmitted to the following:

- navigation indicator (through relay 146F)
- test connector 137Z
- symbol generator box 35A.

### C - Heading and vertical reference reliability

The heading and vertical reference reliability discrete signal, intended for the autopilot, is applied if the following two conditions exist:

- «vertical reference reliability» discrete signal applied,
- system operating in «normal heading» mode. In the case where the system operates in «emergency heading» mode, the following two conditions exist :
  - reliability signal transmitted by the emergency gyromagnetic compass,
- «synchro amplifier» and «watch-dog» discrete signals transmitted by the software of the interface electronics box (see normal heading channel and vertical reference channel).

### 2 - EMERGENCY GYROMAGNETIC COMPASS

When the emergency gyromagnetic compass is operating, it delivers a reliability signal if the following conditions exist:

ΑL

- no anomaly detected by the associated monitoring system

- fast resetting of emergency gyromagnetic heading with respect to magnetic heading completed.

The «CG90 reliability» signal is transmitted to the following:

- navigation indicator (through relay 146F)

- main computer 104F

- interface electronics box 145F

- test connector 40Z.

#### 3 - ROLL/PITCH OUTPUT MULTIPLIER

The «vertical reference reliability» signal is no longer transmitted to test connector 137Z by the monitoring system of the roll/pitch output multiplier when an anomaly is detected in the repeating circuits by this monitoring system.

#### 4 - SPHERICAL INDICATOR

The flag alarm appears on spherical indicator 28F when:

- the «vertical reference reliability» signal generated by interface electronics box 145F is applied,

- a signal is fed by the spherical indicator internal monitoring system.

#### PRINCIPLE OF MARKER BEACON SYSTEM

The Marker beacon system is a radio-navigational facility operating with radio beacons and indicating to the pilot the moment at which the aircraft flies over these radio beacons.

The system includes:

- MARKER beacon receiver 90R
- MARKER beacon selector switch 175R
- MARKER beacon antenna 91R.

The MARKER beacon indicator light, located on spherical indicator 28F, indicates to the pilot the moment at which the aircraft flies over a radio beacon.

Radio selector box 29R is fed with the radio beacon identification signals.

### OPERATION OF MARKER BEACON SYSTEM (Figure 2)

The MARKER beacon receiver operates when the MARKER beacon selector switch is set to «ON». When the aircraft flies over the radio beacon, the receiver receives the 75 MHz RF signals modulated at 400, 1300 or 3000 Hz corresponding to the overflown radio beacon.

The MARKER beacon system signals the moment the aircraft flies over each radio beacon by a double information:

- aural information through the telephone system (radio selector box)

- visual information through the MARKER beacon indicator light on the spherical indicator.

#### The receiver includes:

- RF reception circuits

- circuits detecting and shaping the signal displayed on the spherical indicator.

#### 1 - RF AND DETECTION CIRCUITS

The modulated carrier is filtered and amplified before being transmitted to the detection unit. The detection unit delivers AF information to an audio AF channel which distributes this information to the AF amplifier (for headset) and to the visual indicating channel.

Two oscillators, controlled by the «TEST» position of the MARKER beacon selector switch, simulate the radio beacon signals for the self-test of receivers.

#### 2 - DISPLAY CIRCUITS

The detected AF signal, collected at the output of the audio AF channel preamplifier, is transmitted to the three channels corresponding to the 400, 1300 and 3000 Hz modulation frequencies. Each channel is composed of a selective amplifier, a filter and a relay.

The three channels are interconnected through the aircraft wiring, and channel signals are transmitted to the spherical indicator where they are displayed through the MARKER beacon indicator light.

The audio AF signal is amplified and transmitted to the radio selector box.

#### 3 -- POWER SUPPLY

The 28 V DC power supply of the MARKER beacon receiver is ensured by DC system 2 through fuse-breaker 52R and is controlled from the MARKER beacon selector switch.

A power supply unit, built into the MARKER beacon receiver, generates the 15 V DC voltages required for operation of the receiver.

The contacts of the relays controlling the MARKER beacon indicator light are supplied with 28 V DC power.

### PRINCIPLE OF NAVIGATION SYSTEM (Figure 1)

#### 1 - SCOPE

The purpose of the navigation system is:

- to compute and display the true heading
- to compute and display the steering parameters for the aircraft (distance and true bearing) to a target chosen by the pilot
  - to display the true bearing of a radio-electrical beacon (see 12-8)
  - to display the autopilot heading deviation.

#### 2 - COMPOSITION

The navigation system consists of:

- gyro-accelerometer center 35F and interface electronics box 145F
- Doppler radar 34S
- main computer 104F.

These equipment items are linked up with the control, selection and display units.

#### A - Control and selection units

Navigation control panel 9F determines the operation mode to be used for navigation. It allows for the selection of the navigation target to which various parameters are attached. The latter can be inserted or computed for display.

#### B - Detection units

They are:

- interface electronics box 145F and its flux valve 41F which furnish the magnetic or gyromagnetic heading to symbol generator box 35A and to navigation indicator 68F,
- air data computer 17F which furnishes the aircraft speed and altitude information to symbol generator box 35A and the altitude information to main computer 104F.

#### C - Display units

#### They are:

- spherical indicator 28F
- navigation indicator 68F which displays:
  - the true heading or the gyromagnetic or magnetic heading
  - the steering parameters to a target
  - the autopilot heading deviation
  - the computation faults during the navigation sequence
- head-up display 34A which displays:
  - the gyromagnetic or magnetic heading
  - the altitude
  - the aircraft parameters (indicated airspeed, Mach number)
  - the distance to the navigation target.

This data is displayed through digital values.

The navigation system equipment items are started up through a control located on mode and heading selector panel 37F.

### 4 - MONITORING

The gyro-accelerometer center, the interface electronics box and the main computer are monitored. The failures covered by this monitoring are displayed on the navigation indicator and on the navigation

The navigation indicator receives the failure warning signals from the peripheral equipment items and displays them.

The display depends on the switchings carried out on the navigation indicator.

It comprises a vertical reference gyroscope slaved to the Earth vertical, and a free directional gyroscope.  These gyroscopes stabilize the gimbals which support two horizontal reference accelerometers and one vertical reference accelerometer.  The center can operate: \nin vertical mode: \neither in «inertial» mode in conjunction with the main computer which sends out the precession commands of the vertical reference gyroscope  or in «attitude» mode, in which case the center operates as a self-contained unit with respect to the computer, just like a conventional gyroscopic center; \nin heading mode:  the center operates always in free directional mode.  The center is set to 5° in nose-down direction with regard to the LRL. |                             |     |

#### HIBRID GYRO-INERTIAL NAVIGATION SYSTEM

- GENERAL
- PRINCIPLE OF GYRO-INERTIAL NAVIGATION

{212}------------------------------------------------

4. 4.

{213}------------------------------------------------

![](_page_213_Picture_0.jpeg)

![](_page_213_Picture_2.jpeg)

#### **HYBRID GYRO-INERTIAL NAVIGATION SYSTEM**

#### **GENERAL**

The purpose of the hybrid gyro-inertial navigation system is to provide all data regarding the accelerations, speeds, attitudes and position of the aircraft.

These data are transmitted to the indicators and to the localization, navigation and attack software modules of the main computer (see 10-8).

The gyro-inertial navigation system comprises the following:

- the gyro-accelerometer center, which senses the attitude, heading and acceleration data; these data are transmitted to the main computer through the interface electronics box which sets the information in code form to allow for its use;
- the interface electronics box, which dialogs with the main computer and interchanges the information between the main computer and the gyro-accelerometer center;
- the Doppler radar, which senses the aircraft horizontal speed components and transmits them to the main computer; this horizontal speed is taken as a reference information for computing the updating of data provided by the gyro-accelerometer center and resetting the latter with respect to the local vertical;
- the air data computer; connected with the angle-of-attack probe, it allows the computer to determine the air speed; this speed is used for various operation modes as a reference information for computing the updating of data provided by the gyro-accelerometer center;
- the main computer, which processes the data required for the resetting and updating of the gyro-accelerometer center and for the navigation computation.
- It dialogs with the interface electronics box and computes the data for resetting of the gyro-accelerometer center (comparison of accelerometer speed with reference speed and computation of accelerometer data updating).

Resetting, stabilization and updating of the gyro-accelerometer center through speed information from the Doppler radar or the air data computer, are functions generated by the inertia-Doppler system software.

The gyro-accelerometer center, interface electronics box and main computer assembly allows for carrying out inertial navigation. The updating of the pavigation data provided by this assembly, through data coming from the Doppler radar or the air data system, allows for greatly improved performance.

# MIRAGE F

#### PRINCIPLE OF GYRO-INERTIAL NAVIGATION

#### 1 - INTRODUCTION

As for any navigation procedure, gyro-inertial navigation is intended to determine the position and speed of a moving object.

Since inertia is a mechanical property of matter, expressing its dislike for alteration of its speed condition, let us take an example to introduce this notion of inertia.

Suppose a moving car, with a blind folded passenger sitting next to the driver. This passenger having no visual contact with the outside, let us see to what extent he can reason out the distance covered through the feeling imparted by the car's motion.

An increase in the car's speed will convey to the passenger a feeling of his being pressed firmly against the back of his seat. Conversely, braking by the driver will convey the feeling of his being propelled forward. Add to this that cornering to the right or to the left will cause the passenger to topple over to the left or to the right. We may therefore deduce that, if he be sufficiently responsive, this passenger could in principle carry out the following operations:

- detect the speed variations of the car, through his feeling of being pushed forward or pushed

back,

- detect the changes in direction of the car, also through his feeling of being pushed to the right or to the left.

- deduce the distance covered by means of a number of suitable mathematical operations.

One would say that this passenger is performing inertial navigation, that is localization of the car in relation to the terrain over which it travels, by shrewdly analyzing the forces to which he is subject due to the car's motion.

These forces are mainly in response to the inertia of his body: inertia is characterized by the relation (a constant ratio for each physical body) that exists between these forces and the corresponding speed variations within a given time limit.

Of course, to carry out such a program within the required precision limits would be most difficult, if not impossible, for a human being.

It is however possible for a computer to fulfill this program, with the aid of data provided by high-precision mechanisms and by command mechanisms.

#### 2 - BASIC PRINCIPLES OF GYRO-INERTIAL NAVIGATION (figure 5)

We have just seen that, placed in a vehicle acting as a screen against outside information, it is possible to solve the fundamental navigation problem, that is the knowledge at all times of the vehicle's travel, based solely on the data that can be measured under these conditions: the accelerations to which the vehicle has been submitted and which have been measured by detectors installed in this vehicle (accelerometers).

Computations allowing to determine the distance covered, through data provided by the accelerometers, are carried out by an electronic computer.

### A - MEASUREMENT OF ACCELERATION - DETERMINATION OF SPEED AND DISPLACEMENT

MIRAGE F

MANUAL 12

The function of the computer thus mainly consists in computing the distance covered by double introduction of the time factor into the processing of the measured acceleration signals (introduction of the time factor into the signal processing constitutes the integration of this signal).

As a matter of fact, by definition, the acceleration corresponds to the speed variation within a given period of time; speed itself corresponds to the variation in covered distance within a given period of time. Reciprocally:

-speed is obtained from processing of the acceleration with introduction of the time factor,

i.e. an integration of acceleration;

- the covered distance is obtained from processing of the speed with introduction of the time factor, i.e. an integration of speed.

To conclude, two successive integrations of the acceleration in time lead to the expected result:

- the first integration gives speed,

- the second integration gives the distance covered.

The previous figure gives, for various accelerations, the corresponding values of speeds and displacements.

In an aircraft, the need to know the displacements has led to use two horizontal accelerometers. One is for measuring the accelerations along North-South axis, the other for measuring the accelerations along East-West axis.

So, knowing the aircraft position upon departure, it will be possible to determine its instantaneous position at every moment.

The figure represents the two accelerometric channels associated with the North-South and East-West accelerometers; in the example shown, the aircraft has covered 250 nautical miles northward, and 200 nautical miles eastward since take-off.

### 3 - PRINCIPLE OF ACCELEROMETERS AND NOTION OF ACCELEROMETER READING (figure 6)

#### A - Principle

Placed in a moving body, an accelerometer permits to measure the acceleration undergone by this body along a given axis.

The operation of an accelerometer can be understood by considering it as a mass m sliding without any friction along two parallel and rigid rails whose purpose is to limit the displacements of this mass to a direction X specific to the casing and called axis of sensitivity or axis of entry of the accelerometer. The mass m is linked to the casing by a spring acting as a dynamometer.

Whenever the accelerometer undergoes an acceleration  $\gamma$  along its axis of sensitivity, the mass m is subjected to a force  $F=m\gamma$  whose direction is opposite to the acceleration. Such force causes a displacement of the mass resulting in an extension (or compression) of the spring which then exerts a return force Fr=Kx (where x represents the displacement of «m» and k the coefficient of elongation of the spring); a balance between these two forces is expressed by F=Fr, i.e.  $m\gamma=kx$ .

Hence it is possible to deduct :  $x = \frac{m}{k} \gamma$ .

Since  $\frac{M}{k}$  is a permanent ratio for a given accelerometer, one simply has to measure the displacement x to obtain a signal proportional to the acceleration.

NOTE: It should be remembered that, when an accelerometer is subjected to acceleration along any direction at variance with its axis of sensitivity, it will detect only the acceleration projection exerted along its axis of sensitivity.

Restricted

B - Applying the principle of accelerometers to gyro-inertial navigation

Two accelerometers mounted on a gyro-inertial platform measure: the one, accelerations of the bearer vehicle following the North-South ground axis; the other, accelerations of the bearer vehicle following the East-West ground axis.

However, should one of these accelerometers not be absolutely perpendicular to the local gravity field, it will detect the projection of the Earth's gravitational force (mg) on its axis of sensitivity. This means that an acceleration cannot be measured directly by means of an accelerometer, since it cannot mark the difference between inertia forces (function of the acceleration) and Earth gravity force. And so, the concept of accelerometer reading. Therefore, to usefully digest an accelerometer reading one should:

- either know precisely, on the one hand, the directions of the accelerometer axes of sensitivity in relation to gravity and on the other hand, know with great precision the gravity import at a given spot,
- or make use of a system which will hold the accelerometers in a horizontal plane and thereby break free from gravity. It is this last solution which has been retained.

#### 4 - INSTALLATION OF ACCELEROMETERS ON A STABILIZED PLATFORM

A - Platform suspension (figure 6-B)

Whatever be the attitude of the aircraft in which they are installed, the accelerometers must be able to retain their horizontal position.

To this end, it is required to install them on a support called a platform, connected with the aircraft through a gimbal device which provides freedom from the aircraft's movements.

- B Platform stabilization (figure 7-A)
- (1) Purpose of the gyroscope in a gyro-inertial center.

To retain the platform in a horizontal position, one uses one of the essential characteristics of the gyroscope : its fixed position relative to space.

If one has a top spinning at high speed, with its pin hung by gimbals, this pin will remain fixed in space in relation to the stars. This fundamental characteristic is in fact the very first characteristic of the gyroscope.

(2) Preparation principle for stabilizing a platform.

The figure shows how to stabilize a platform using a gyroscope.

Accelerometers and gyroscopes are installed together on this platform. Assuming that this platform is initially horizontal in relation to an axis, if some disturbance causes it to swerve from this neutral position, the axis of the gyroscope will remain firm. However, the gyro box will move and the import of this travel will be translated into a signal by a sensor integrated with the gyro.

![](_page_220_Picture_20.jpeg)

{221}------------------------------------------------

![](_page_221_Picture_1.jpeg)

![](_page_221_Picture_2.jpeg)

This signal is then amplified and sent to a slaving motor which will bring the assembly back to the initial stable position. In this configuration, the accelerometer will again perform normally and since it no longer detects any component brought about by gravity, it will solely measure the horizontal accelerations to which it is subjected in flight.

NOTE: This figure is simplified. It shows a stabilized platform, relative to a single axis, whereas the deviations must be measured in relation to two axes : roll, pitch.

Accelerometer stabilization must also occur around the yaw axis of the platform, so that these accelerometers may retain a known fixed direction.

NOTE: This type of system provides a vertical reference which may very well take the place of that given by a conventional vertical gyro.

C - Horizontal stabilization for navigation at ground level : automated system.

The aircraft bearing this platform moves in relation to Earth which is spherical and revolves on its axis.

The tendency of the platform, with its previously described stabilization device, is thus to be slaved to a fixed direction in relation to space. This will cause it to lose its initial horizontal position in relation to Earth.

One must therefore take these two factors into account : spherical Earth and its revolving around its axis, so as to be able to apply the corresponding corrections.

The automated system will thus modify the orientation of the platform as a function of these corrections so as to maintain it in horizontal position in relation with Earth (that is, perpendicular to the local vertical).

(1) Balancing due to the rotation of Earth (figure 7-B)

Take the case of a parked aircraft with its gyro-inertial system operating.

Let us then consider an observer located over the pole and looking at the gyro-inertial center located, let us suppose at the equator.

Now let us observe what happens if we apply no correction (the platform itself remaining in position on the equator):

- at the instant T the platform has been set level,

- at the instant T  $\pm$   $\Delta t$  for example, the gyroscope has well maintained the orientation, but the platform is no longer perpendicular to the local vertical.

To counteract this inconvenience, the platform is compelled to take its bearing around its North-South axis in proportion to the Earth rotation ratio so as to keep it at all times, perpendicular to the local vertical.

NOTE: As this correction is applied around a North-South axis, it should be noted that for a platform located elsewhere than on the equator, this correction is a function of the latitude; at the extreme, for a platform located at the pole, this correction would be zero.

{222}------------------------------------------------

![](_page_222_Picture_0.jpeg)

### MIRAGE F

MANUAL 12

(2) Compensation caused by displacement of Earth's surface (curvature of the Earth) (figure 7-C)

Restricted

If we now consider the instance of an aircraft moving on the surface of the Earth, the local vertical will show a shift from its position at departure point.

Correction of the vertical can then be carried out in a fashion similar to that caused by the Earth's rotation.

If no correction is applied, since the platform maintains a fixed course in relation to space, it will seem to bank in relation to the aircraft, between the moment of its take-off and that of its landing.

The correction forces the platform to take its bearing so that the accelerometers will retain their horizontal position throughout the flight.

It will therefore be necessary that the platform rotate at the rotation speed of the aircraft in relation to Earth; the platform will otherwise remain parallel to itself and when the local vertical has turned by an angle A, the accelerometers will likewise make this angle A with the horizontal position.

This rotation speed equals:

$$\omega = V \times \frac{1}{R}$$

with V = speed of the aircraft R = radius of the Earth

NOTE: This correction is applicable along the two axes North-South and East-West.

(3) Introduction of correction commands: precession command (figure 7-D)

Thus, to maintain a platform constantly in a horizontal position, its orientation will have to be modified as a function of the corrections dealt with previously. This is done by shifting the axes of the gyroscopes. This correction is known as the precession command.

To this end, the various corrections are added, converted into signals and sent to the torque motors of the gyroscopes involved. The gyroscopes precess; these precessions once detected are in turn converted into signals. These signals are amplified and sent on to the slaving motors which modify the platform attitudes in proportion to the values of these corrective signals, as in the case of stabilization.

- D Orientation of the platform in azimuth (figure 8)
- (1) Need for a defined platform orientation

To obtain latitude and longitude data from accelerometric measurements, one should know precisely the orientation of the accelerometers in relation to the North-South and East-West axes of the Earth.

This orientation must not vary as a function of the yawing of the aircraft. It is therefore required to stabilize the platform in azimuth.

MIRAGE F

One should remember that an accurate knowledge of the orientation of the platform in azimuth is also needed to correctly distribute the automated system commands: if these corrections were to be distributed in haphazard fashion, the platform would soon loose its horizontality.

#### (2) Determination of platform orientation in azimuth (figure 8-A)

When setting the gyro-accelerometer center in operation, the platform orientation is determined in relation to the geographic North.

This operation completed, the platform is left free and stabilized in azimuth, that is, it retains its orientation in relation to space and not in relation to landmarks.

Thus when one moves along the path AB, the axis of the North-South accelerometer shifts gradually in relation to the North due to this travel and to the Earth's rotation, thereby forming an angle  $\alpha$ .

As the computer knows the value of the Earth's rotation and the latitude of the spot, it computes constantly the value of this angle  $\alpha$ . The orientation of the center is therefore known at all times. It can therefore compute the accelerations according to the North-South and East-West ground

axes and correctly dispatch the required corrections to the automated system.

#### (3) Results affecting the position computation (figure 8-B)

The North-South and East-West accelerometers are not systematically set to Earth North-South and East-West directions. The computer will therefore proceed with an axis change operation through use of the computed accelerations, so as to obtain the accelerations in accordance with the Earth North-South and East-West axes.

#### 5 - PLATFORM ALIGNMENT (figure 9)

In the line of argument developped up to now, it has been accepted that the equipment was lined up and ready for operation. In reality, when starting up a gyro-inertial center, two essential operations have to be performed:

- the accelerometers have to be leveled (platform in horizontal position)
- the platform must be set in relation to the true North (or geographic North).

#### A - Search for horizontal position

During alignment, the platform is set in horizontal position by making use of data provided by erectors which detect the visible vertical (thus the need for the aircraft to remain motionless during alignment).

#### B - Determining the platform orientation in relation to the North

Orientation of the directional gyroscope axis is to be compared to a true heading information (provided by a transfer gyroscope or inserted by the pilot). This allows to find the initial azimuth angle  $\alpha$  of the platform, angle from which all gyro-inertial navigation computations will be carried out.

#### 6 - OPERATION SYNTHESIS

In short, a gyro-inertial center installed in an aircraft functions as follows: the dual integration of acceleration measured by two accelerometers, provides the distance covered, and then the position (latitude, longitude).

These accelerometers must be perfectly horizontal:

- whatever be the attitude of the bearer aircraft: thus their installation on a platform hung on gimbals and their stabilization by means of gyroscopes;

- whatever be the position and the travel speed at ground level: thus, the automated system which permanently resets the horizontality of the platform, while recording the fact that the aircraft is located and travels over an approximately spherical Earth rotating about its polar axis. This automated system correction is computed from speed and position data, which in turn are prepared on the basis of accelerometric information.

Starting-up of a gyro-inertial center presupposes perfect horizontality and accurate setting of the accelerometers in relation to the North, and so an alignment phase.

To improve the system's performance, its indications are updated through use of data provided by another source. Indeed, pure inertial information will drift in the long run. It must therefore be reset in relation to a more permanent information.

This is the function of the Doppler radar system. Updating is computed through the error between the pure inertial navigation North and East speeds (detected by the accelerometers) and the North and East speeds computed from the horizontal speed provided by the Doppler radar.

The error signal allows:

- in the short run, and after major amplification, to correct the vertical setting of the vertical reference gyroscope of the gyro-accelerometer center,

- in the long run, to correct the pure inertial speed information (acquired by integration of the accelerometers).

A similar updating, even though less precise, can be carried out through aerodynamic speed information. But it should only be put to use if the Doppler radar information is unavailable.

1

{227}------------------------------------------------

![](_page_227_Picture_0.jpeg)

MIRAGE F

MANUAL 12

#### 7 - POSITIONING IN VERTICAL PLANE - PRINCIPLE OF BARO-INERTIAL ALTITUDE

The information usually recorded for gyro-inertial navigation involves essentially horizontal plane navigation. Yet, in addition to the two horizontal accelerometers, the platform is also equipped with a vertical accelerometer.

A rapid increase of errors in the vertical plane as a function of the altitude will prevent attaining pure inertial speed in this plane (that is to say attained solely through the integration of accelerometric measures).

Thus, in the case of long term maneuvers of the aircraft in vertical plane, one is led to substitute inertial altitude information by barometric altitude information.

Inversely, in the case of short term maneuvers, inertial altitude information becomes a leading factor. Indeed, since the inertial information response time is sharply lower than the barometric aneroid response time, a greater altitude reading precision is attained while the aircraft travels at high speed in a vertical plane.

It is thus mainly during bombing and ground attack that information provided by the vertical accelerometer is necessary.

In short, the baro-inertial altitude is a result of the combination of a barometric and an accelerometric information: this baro-inertial altitude is reset, in the long run, in relation to the barometric information; in the short run, the accelerometric information is added thereto.

### OPERATION OF GYRO-ACCELEROMETER CENTER INERTIA-DOPPLER SOFTWARE

#### 1 - DESCRIPTION (figure 9)

The gyro-accelerometer center comprises fundamentally a vertical reference gyroscope and two gimbals positioned as follows :

- a roll gimbal which pivots about an axis parallel to the longitudinal axis of the aircraft; the roll gimbal is supported by the mount of that center;

- a pitch gimbal positioned within the roll gimbal; it pivots about an axis parallel to the cross-axis of the aircraft and supports the vertical reference gyroscope;

- the pitch gimbal is slaved to the vertical by the vertical reference gyroscope;

- in addition to the vertical reference gyroscope, the pitch gimbal supports a directional gyroscope which comprises an elevation gimbal and an azimuth gimbal; the elevation gimbal is slaved so as to remain perpendicular to the azimuth gimbal; this allows the directional gyroscope rotor axis to remain in horizontal position;

- two accelerometers are positioned on the azimuth gimbal of the directional gyroscope.

- a third accelerometer is positioned on the pitch gimbal of the center.

Due to the slaving of the pitch gimbal of the center to the vertical and the positioning of the directional gyroscope within this pitch gimbal, we have again the conditions allowing to consider inertial navigation, in accordance with the theory treated earlier:

the accelerometers are attached to a support hung by gimbals. This provides them with the mechanical possibility to be held horizontally notwithstanding the motion of the aircraft. They are stabilized horizontally by means of the vertical reference gyroscope and through slaving to the pitch gimbal. They are stabilized in azimuth by the directional gyroscope.

- the horizontal gyroscopes evaluate the acceleration components of the aircraft from the direction of their axis of sensitivity. The dual integration of their output signal provides first the distance covered by the aircraft, and then its position.

- the horizontality of these accelerometers is corrected as a function of the position and of the speed of travel of the aircraft. This correction is computed through speed and position data themselves generated from accelerometric data. These corrections are applied to the torque motors of the vertical reference gyroscope.

#### 2 - AUTOMATED SYSTEM : SLAVING OF ROLL/PITCH GIMBALS (figure 10)

#### A - Slaving of roll/pitch gimbals to vertical reference gyroscope

Any orientation deviation between the pitch gimbal and the rotation axis of the vertical reference gyroscope rotor is detected by sensors. The sensors comprise an energization coil, located on the lower gimbal of the vertical reference gyroscope, and detector coils integral with the pitch gimbal.

Assuming that the rotor axis be in vertical position, any deviation sensed would indicate that either the rotation axis of the pitch gimbal is not horizontal or that the pitch gimbal itself is not vertical.

The resetting motor M101 will correct the lack of horizontality of the rotation axis of the pitch gimbal, until cancellation of the transverse deviation. The resetting motor M201 will correct the lack of vertical of the pitch gimbal, until cancellation of the longitudinal deviation.

{231}------------------------------------------------

![](_page_231_Picture_2.jpeg)

![](_page_231_Picture_3.jpeg)

FIGURE 9 -- FUNCTIONAL DIAGRAM OF GYRO-ACCELEROMETER CENTER-INSTALLATION OF ACCELEROMETERS

{232}------------------------------------------------

![](_page_232_Picture_0.jpeg)

MIRAGE F

B - Principle of slaving of vertical reference gyroscope to the vertical

The system functions in two steps to hold the rotation axis of the vertical reference gyroscope rotor to the vertical.

On the ground, with the aircraft immobile, the direction of the local vertical is given to the system during an operation phase called alignment. The rotation axis of the rotor is slaved to this direction.

After alignment, the automated system commands reset the gyroscope to the computed orientation of the local vertical.

C - Slaving of vertical reference gyroscope during alignment

The direction of the local vertical is provided by means of two mercury erectors located on the pitch gimbal. They sense any deviation of the pitch gimbal in relation to the vertical and control the vertical reference gyroscope torque motors. Taking into account the slaving of the pitch gimbal to the vertical reference gyroscope, balance is attained when the vertical reference gyroscope axis is actually vertical.

NOTE: To shorten the time needed for realignment of the system to the vertical, the gimbals are set to their synchro zero prior to being aligned, with the erectors.

D - Slaving of vertical reference gyroscope after alignment

After alignment, the vertical reference gyroscope orientation is corrected as a function of the local vertical changes. This is automation. It is computed through the travel of the aircraft and the Earth rotation.

The import of the correction imposed by the travel of the aircraft depends on its travel speeds following North and East directions. The import of the correction imposed due to Earth rotation depends on the latitude.

The computed corrections are shared out amongst the torque motors of the vertical reference gyroscope as a function of its gimbal orientation relative to true North, that is to say function of true heading.

NOTE: In emergency mode, the vertical correction is generated by the mercury erectors.

3- ORIENTATION OF ACCELEROMETERS IN AZIMUTH : determination of angle  $\alpha$  (figure 11)

A — Principle for computation of angle  $\alpha$ 

The pitch gimbal (therefore the accelerometer support) is kept slaved to Earth vertical. The axes of sensitivity of accelerometers 1 and 2 are horizontal. To free their orientation from the aircraft yawing, their support is stabilized in azimuth by the directional gyroscope which determines a directional reference in the horizontal plane.

To compute the North and East travels of the aircraft, the computer has to know the direction of this directional reference relative to true North. It functions in two steps.

On the ground, with the aircraft immobile, during an alignment phase, the system determines the initial orientation  $\alpha$  i of the accelerometers in relation to true North from a true heading information THi given to the system. The computer knows the orientation of the azimuth gimbal of the directional gyroscope in relation to the pitch gimbal (angle  $\Psi p$ ). It deduces there from  $\alpha$ i ( $\alpha$ i = THi +  $\Psi p$ ). This value is stored.

After alignment, the directional gyroscope is left free in azimuth. The computer computes the change of  $\alpha$  from  $\alpha$ i as a function of the aircraft position and the change of this position.

#### B - Nature of initial true heading THi data

The initial true heading can be given to the system in several ways:

- either by a transfer gyro : the heading data can be immediately put to use or stored for later use ;
- or manually by the pilot after orientation of the aircraft to a known geographic axis (for example a runway axis);
- or from the magnetic heading given through the magnetic heading channel and from the variation that has been stored or is fed by the pilot.

#### C - Initial alignment procedure

(1) Modes in which the initial true heading THi has initially been given by the transfer gyro. This is the normal way in which the system is used.

When the transfer gyro is mounted in the aircraft, the computer receives the initial true heading which it stores in a volatile memory, if the mode selector of MHSP is set to «ALN-TRF». This corresponds to an immediate utilization of the data THi. If the mode selector of MHSP is on «ST-BY», the initial true heading is stored in a non-volatile memory. The data is then available for later use, even when the electrical supply of the system has been switched off.

If one knows the actual true heading of the aircraft, it is possible to introduce it directly into the memory by writing through the NCP.

The initial true heading THi is registered by the computer when one presses «TRF» button of the MHSP.

The registered initial true heading is the one introduced directly into the volatile memory by the transfer gyroscope, when the mode selector of MHSP is set to «ALN-TRF». It is the one stored by the non-volatile memory when the mode selector is set to «ALN-STR». In the latter case, it is important that the aircraft be perfectly oriented to the stored heading, when pressing the «TRF» button.

As the computer knows the platform azimuth  $\Psi p$  and initial true heading THi values, it may then determine the angle  $\alpha i$ .

(2) Modes in which the initial true heading THi has not been initially given. These are called MAG-VAR modes.

As long as «TRF» button of MHSP has not been depressed, the system will function in these modes.

The initial true heading is determined from a gyromagnetic heading GMH and from the stored variation MV: THi = GMH + MV.

The computer will then determine, as done earlier, the initial setting angle  $\alpha i$  of the accelerometers.

The variation MV may be modified by the pilot from the NCP. It is never computed while a «MAG-VAR» mode is being used.

These modes are possible only if the magnetic monitoring channel operates correctly, the true heading TH2 used for navigation computation being obtained through the gyromagnetic heading GMH and through the stored variation MV (see 10-8, «Localization»).

In these modes, indicator light «M-VAR» of the NCP remains on.

# HYBRIDIZATION OF GYRO-INERTIAL SYSTEM INERTIA-DOPPLER SOFTWARE (Figure 12)

#### 1 - PRINCIPLE

The speed data supplied by the gyro-inertial system previously described is accurate in the short run. However this data is subject to errors.

On the one hand, these errors are liable to increase with time.

On the other, any error, as small as it may be, in one of the initial conditions, will cause an oscillation situation which in turn will produce constantly increasing errors.

Thus one is led to couple the gyro-inertial system to a system which, on the one hand, will damp these oscillations and on the other, will curtail the errors in the long run.

To avoid an increase of errors after a long period of time, the gyro-inertial system is coupled to a navigation system which provides accurate long-term speed data. This speed data is used as reference.

The coupling consists in slaving the speed obtained from the accelerometers to the reference speed. It also allows updating of the gyro-inertial system.

#### 2 - APPLICATION

The speed data obtained from the accelerometers is compared with the reference speed.

The difference between these two data corrects the accelerometric output of the gyro center (updating) and resets the vertical of the center (reduction of long-term errors, especially those caused by the vertical reference gyroscope drift).

The speed signal coming from the first integrator of each channel (North and East) constitutes the so-called «hybrid» speed data which is characterized as follows: in the long run, it practically equals the reference speed; in the short run, data secured from the accelerometers is superimposed on it.

The reference speed is provided either by the Doppler radar or by the air data computer.

Depending on the utilization conditions for the aircraft, three main modes of coupling are available:

- inertia-Doppler coupling
- inertia-air data coupling
- pure inertia : no coupling.

#### 3 - INERTIA-DOPPLER COUPLING

This is the basic mode for operation of the system.

Let us remember that the Doppler radar measures the ground speed of the aircraft.

This ground speed remains accurate over a long period of time. In the short run, its value may be marred by appreciable errors in transit mode, during maneuvers of the aircraft or fast variations in the ground speed.

6 - 143

{240}------------------------------------------------

![](_page_240_Picture_0.jpeg)

### MIRAGE F

MANUAL 12

We do thus notice that the gyro-inertial navigation system and the Doppler navigation system have complementary qualities which allow to take the fullest advantage of each other's information, within the mixed system that has been considered in the previous paragraphs.

Restricted

- Gyro-inertial navigation : good accuracy in the short run, major errors in the long run,

- Doppler navigation : poor accuracy in the short run, good accuracy in the long run.

This leads us to take the Doppler speed as reference for resetting of the inertial speed.

The Doppler radar provides Doppler frequencies according to the transmission beam paths. The computer determines the ground speed and provides the coupling of the inertial data.

#### 4 - INERTIA-AIR DATA COUPLING

The air speed ASI data provided by the air data computer offers the same long-term accuracy and short-term inaccuracy characteristics as the Doppler speed.

It is therefore used, after adjustment, as reference when coupling it with the gyro-inertial data is intended.

The air data computer provides the computer with the air speed ASI module. By using the angle-of-attack, the computer will establish the vector AS (magnitude and direction). Adjustment as a function of the roll and pitch angles will allow to achieve the value of the horizontal projection.

Since the air speed is the speed of the aircraft within the air mass, the wind speed should be added, to achieve the ground speed.

The relative inaccuracy of this last value provides less satisfactory accuracy for the data secured through inertia-air data coupling than for the data obtained through inertia-Doppler coupling.

The computer ensures the coupling with the inertial data, in accordance with the principle enunciated earlier.

#### 5 - PURE INERTIA

No correction is to be applied to the data provided by or computed from the gyro-accelerometer center signals.

In the long run, the quality of the various data that have been obtained will diminish.

Thus, the pure inertia mode is not to be used over an extended period.

### 6 - SPECIAL COUPLING CASES

A certain number of operation particularities are to be found, which correspond to each of the coupling modes. These particularities are subject to the aircraft conditions, the availability of sensors enabling the coupling and the presence of jammer transmission.

Restricted

- On the ground, with the aircraft motionless during alignment, there is not truly a coupling mode : in fact it is a matter of initialization of the system operation.

- On the ground, after alignment: taxiing mode.

The system enters the «emergency taxiing» mode when it receives the «CG90 reliability» data.

The system operates in inertia-air data mode with air speed forced to zero.

- Normal taxiing mode.

The system enters the «normal taxiing» mode when it receives the «platform alignment completed» information (setting to local vertical by means of the mercury erectors and transferred true heading). The system operates in inertia-Doppler mode with Doppler speed forced to zero.

- Take-off mode

The system enters this mode when the registered acceleration is greater than 0.2 g or the inertial speed greater that 90 km/h. It operates in pure inertia.

- Flight mode

The system enters this mode when it receives the «U/C shock absorbers extended data». This is the normal operation mode for the system. It is examined in detail in the following paragraph.

- Chopped mode

This is a special flight mode which corresponds to the use of the Remora jammer. In such a situation, the Doppler radar is useless as long as the jammer continues its transmission. The system operates in what is called a chopped mode:

- for a time t1, the computer inhibits the Remora transmission and authorizes Doppler reception to be taken into account; the inertia-Doppler coupling is normal, with a set of different factors:

- for a time t2, the computer authorizes the Remora transmission and inhibits Doppler reception; the inertia-Doppler coupling proceeds with the same set of factors as in the previous case, with a Doppler speed equal to the average of the speed during cycle t1.

- Unavailability of sensors

Should the Doppler be unavailable (out of range, failure) and the air data computer likewise, the corresponding coupling is not possible.

02 - 80

{243}------------------------------------------------

![](_page_243_Picture_0.jpeg)

MIRAGE F MANUAL 12

#### 7 - FLIGHT MODES (figure 13)

A — Scope of hybridization mode switching logic.

The inertia-Doppler coupling is the basic mode. When operating in this mode, we will assume that the system is functioning with optimum accuracy.

When, for whatever reason it may be, Doppler data cannot be used, a switching logic will permit the choice of a coupling mode (or the absence of coupling) to provide the greatest accuracy possible.

This switching logic must therefore assess the accuracy value of the system, compare it with the accuracy that the other modes may provide and switch to another mode if deemed to give a better performance.

B - Accuracy assessment of each coupling mode

In pure inertia mode, degradation of navigation accuracy as a function of time, and in relation to optimum operating mode, is depicted as a parabole reduced to three segments of a straight line.

In Remora mode, as also in inertia-air data mode, degradation of accuracy is considered constant in relation to optimum operating mode. The inertia-air data mode however, provides less satisfactory performance than the Remora mode.

The contents of a counter is associated with each value of the accuracy degradation.

• The zero value characterizes absence of accuracy degradation : such is the case for the inertia-Doppler coupling mode, which has been in use for quite some time, as it corresponds to an optimum operation of the system.

- In pure inertia mode, the counter contents are incremented: its contents evolve as a function of

time, and are depicted as a parabole reduced to three segments of a straight line.

- In Remora mode, the value 2500 characterizes the accuracy degradation. The Remora coupling has been in use for quite some time.

- In inertia-air data mode, the value 5000 characterizes the accuracy degradation.

Let us assume, that at the start, the counter contents equal zero. This corresponds to a situation in which the inertia-Doppler mode has been in use for quite some time.

Should the inertia-Doppler coupling have to be abandoned, two possibilities arise :

- the inertia-Doppler is abandoned due to a Remora transmission. In which case the system goes over to the most performing mode; this will be the pure inertia mode; the counter is incremented; passage to 2500 on the counter leads to abandonment of the pure inertia mode in favor of the better performing chopped mode;

the inertia-Doppler coupling is abandoned due to faulty operation of the Doppler radar (out of range, failure); the system switches to the pure inertia mode; the counter is incremented; passage to 5000 on the counter leads to abandonment of the pure inertia mode in favor of the better perfor-

ming inertia-air data mode.

ALL.

{244}------------------------------------------------

![](_page_244_Picture_0.jpeg)

MIRAGE F

#### C - Disincrementation of counter

When a system is able to switch from one coupling mode to another mode offering better performance, the maximum efficiency of the coupling is reached only after some time. This means that a return to optimum precision for the new coupling mode is not immediate. Which has the following repercussions for the counter disincrementation law:

- when the system switches back to the inertia-Doppler mode, disincrementation of the counter to zero (return to optimum performance) is accomplished following an exponential decreasing in relation to time; this exponential is reduced to two segments of a straight line;

- when the system switches back to the Remora mode, the counter disincrementation is accomplished following a straight line decreasing in relation to time until it reaches the 2500 scale.

This disincrementation law allows at all time for correspondence between the navigation data accuracy degradation and the counter contents. This is required when, during a transition phase of returning to a coupling which offers better performance, the conditions once again impose a coupling corresponding to a more degraded coupling mode: the precision of the system has not reached its optimum value and the degradation in the new mode has to cumulate with the remains of the accuracy degradation due to the operation in the preceding coupling modes.

#### D - Operation example

The figure shows a situation in which the system has been operating for some time in inertia-Doppler mode.

At t = 0 time, starting of Remora. The system switches to pure inertia. The counter increments itself.

At t = 3 min 20 s, the counter reaches 2500. The system switches to chopped mode.

At  $t=5\,$  min, the Doppler is out of range ant the Doppler data is unavailable. The system switches to pure inertia. The counter increments itself.

At t=6 min, the Doppler data is again available. The system switches to chopped mode. The counter disincrements to 2500 and remains at 2500.

At t=8 min, the Remora ceases transmission. The system switches to inertia-Doppler. The counter disincrements.

At t = 9 min 35 s, the Doppler is no longer valid. The system switches to pure inertia and the counter increments itself.

At t = 13 min 45 s, the counter reaches 5000. The system switches to inertia-air data. The counter remains at 5000.

At t = 14 min 40 s, the Doppler data is again available. The system switches to inertia-Doppler. The counter disincrements to zero and remains at zero.

#### RECAPITULATION OF VARIOUS OPERATING MODES OF THE SYSTEM AND OF VARIOUS DATA SUPPLIED

#### ALIGNMENT

The position is supplied to the system.

#### **EMERGENCY TAXIING**

It occurs when the CG90 supplies a «CG90 reliability» signal.

- The true heading is generated from the CG90 gyromagnetic heading and from the magnetic variation stored or displayed by the pilot.
  - The speed data for navigation computations are forced to zero.
  - The altitude provided is a barometric altitude.
  - The system operates in inertia-air data mode with an air speed forced to zero.

#### MAG-VAR TAXIING

It occurs when the «end of platform alignment» data is available.

- The true heading is generated from the gyromagnetic heading and from the magnetic variation stored or displayed by the pilot (TH2).
  - The speed data for navigation computations are forced to zero.
  - The position is forced on the alignment position.
  - The altitude provided is the baro-inertial altitude.
  - The wind is forced to nil value.
  - The system operates in inertia-Doppler mode with a Doppler speed forced to zero.

#### **NORMAL TAXIING**

This mode is identical to the MAG-VAR taxiing mode, excepting that the true heading has been supplied to the system at alignment time. The system provides a true heading independently of the gyromagnetic heading (TH1).

The difference between MAG-VAR modes and normal modes reflects on all other modes : for MAG-VAR modes, the true heading has not been inserted in the system. It is generated during take-off and flight modes from a gyromagnetic heading reading and from the stored magnetic variation. For the other modes called normal modes, the true heading is directly generated from the gyroaccelerometer center data.

#### NORMAL TAKE-OFF

This operation mode occurs when the longitudinal acceleration is greater than 0.2 g or when the inertial speed registers more than 90 km/h.

- The true heading is generated from the gyro-accelerometer center data.
- The speed data are obtained from pure inertial data.
- The position is forced on the alignment position.
- The altitude provided is the baro-inertial altitude.
- The system operates in pure inertia.

#### NORMAL FLIGHT

This mode occurs when the U/C shock absorber is extended.

- The true heading is generated from the gyro-accelerometer center data.
- The speed and position data are computed in normal operation by a system operating in inertia-Doppler mode.

# NAVIGATION CONTROL PANEL (NCP) AND MODE AND HEADING SELECTOR PANEL (MHSP) (Figures 17 and 18)

#### 1 - PRESENTATION

The navigation control panel (NCP) and the mode and heading selector panel (MHSP) are two complementary assemblies combining navigation and control functions for the inertia-Doppler navigation system.

#### 2 - NAVIGATION CONTROL PANEL

A -- Description of the front face of the NCP

The front face of the NCP comprises:

- a parameter selector (1) with ten positions performing the following functions :
- DEST (destination). It permits reading and/or insertion of the coordinates (in degrees, minutes and 1/10 minute) of a navigation or bombing target corresponding to the number of the «DATA» counter (LH window : latitude ; RH window : longitude).
- OFS (offset)  $\Delta L/\Delta G$ . It permits reading and/or insertion of the coordinate variations (in minutes, 1/10, 1/100 and 1/1000 minute) in latitude (LH window) and in longitude (RH window) in relation to the navigation or bombing target displayed on the «DATA» counter.
- OFS (offset) P- $\theta$ . It permits reading and/or insertion of the coordinate variations in distance P (LH window : in km and 1/10 km) and in true bearing  $\theta$  (RH window : in degrees and 1/10 degree) in relation to the navigation or bombing target displayed on the «DATA» counter.
- $H/\Delta H$ . It permits reading and/or insertion of the target altitude and altitude difference of the initial position in relation to the target displayed on the «DATA» counter or «mark» altitude (LH window : altitude in meters ; RH window : altitude difference in meters).
- HDG/MVAR. It permits reading and/or insertion of the true heading HDG (LH window in degrees, 1/10 and 1/100 degree) and the magnetic variation (RH window in degrees and 1/10 degree).

02 - 80

#### Restricted

- PP/MRK (present position - marking). It permits reading of the present position or of a «mark» position in geographical coordinates (LH window: latitude; RH window: longitude, in degrees, minutes and 1/10 minute). In this position, the normal display is the present position. This position is frozen as soon as a «mark» has been performed. Present position reappears as soon as the «mark» coordinates have been inserted (by pressing «ENT») or cancelled (by pressing «CAN»).

- UPD/RNG/BRG (update - range - bearing). It permits reading of the horizontal distance and of the true bearing from the present position of the aircraft to any navigation target displayed on the «DATA» counter.

When updating a position (by pressing on control stick button or on «POS» of NCP), the display will show the true bearing and distance variations between the computed position and the true position of the aircraft. These variations will be erased as soon as the updating is validated or invalidated.

The distance is displayed in the LH window (in km and 1/10 km) and the true bearing in the RH window (in degrees and 1/10 degree).

- WD-WS (wind direction - wind speed). It permits reading and/or insertion of the wind direction (LH window in degrees) and speed (RH window in km/h).

- TRK-GS (track - ground speed). It permits reading of the horizontal speed (RH window in km/h) and true course (LH window in degrees and 1/10 degree).

 MON (monitor). Reading or writing of coded data. Permits to read various monitoring and control data for the system, and to read or modify stored data of the main computer.

When on «MON», the main computer sends the six digits of the system status word into the RH window. The three LH digits represent the failure status of the first equipment and the three RH digits that of the second equipment. In each group:

the two LH digits give the equipment number,

- the last digit gives the type of failure.

AVIONS MARCEL DASSAUL BREGUET AVIATION

The NCP interface and data processing circuits of the main computer permit, subject to the NCP commands:

- to introduce the parameters for navigation use,
- to display on demand various flight and navigation parameters provided by the navigation system,
- to monitor the operation of the inertia-Doppler system,
- to select the navigation target number which will be used.

The NCP and the main computer dialog through the digibus. It also receives discrete signals related to navigation system status and operating modes.

The NCP fulfills three functions:

- an interface function : shaping of the data exchanged,
- a data management function: computation and storage of navigation computation data,
- a display function: display of data according to the selected parameter on the parameter selector.

Data coming from or directed to the digibus are managed by the NCP computation system. Every 20 ms, the NCP sends out its validity status word to the main computer. Every 200 ms, the main computer sends the navigation parameter display data to the NCP.

It sends out its commands as a function of the position of its various controls. These commands specify the operation to be carried out by the navigation system, depending on the parameter selector position and the target number selected : command for display on NCP or writing in memory of the main computer.

These data are contained in a word directed to the main computer and sent out by the NCP.

- Where a display command is involved, the NCP sends out the control word. The main computer selects the memory address which contains the requested data, carries out the operation and sends the result to the NCP, after shaping. The NCP receives this data, checks the validity of the address and displays the result.
- Where a main computer memory writing is involved, the data is typed into the NCP which sends out a word corresponding with the typed data. The main computer will make use of this data only after transmission of the carrier word of the introduction command.

The data processing circuits of the NCP also permit other functions:

- updating of the position
- updating of the altitude.

The operation of the NCP is controlled from the mode and heading selector panel.

#### 3 - MODE AND HEADING SELECTOR PANEL (MHSP)

A - Description of the front face of the MHSP

The front face of the MHSP is divided into four separate parts:

(1) A part depending on mode selection:

It comprises:

- A seven-position mode selector (1) fulfilling the following functions :
- TEST: allows to carry out an overall ground test of the navigation system, that is, a simulated internal navigation. It allows to check the reliability of the various elements of the navigation computation and display channel.
  - OFF : allows to switch off the various navigation channel equipment items.
  - STBY: (standby) which allows:
    - to start the navigation control panel, the main computer, the navigation indicator;

- heating of the gyro-accelerometer center;

- introduction of navigation data by means of the NCP;

- to store in the main computer the initial true heading generated by the transfer gyroscope; this stored initial true heading will be used during alignment on the stored true heading.
  - ALN-TRF (alignment transfer), which allows:
- to start the equipment used in navigation (navigation control panel, navigation indicator, main computer, Doppler radar, spherical indicator, interface electronics box, roll-pitch output multiplier, navigation output multiplier, gyro-accelerometer center);

- heating of the gyro-accelerometer center;

- introduction of navigation data into the main computer by means of the NCP;
- introduction of the initial true heading generated by the transfer gyroscope during the alignment with transfer gyroscope.
  - ALN-STR (alignment store), which allows :
    - to start the equipment used in navigation (see ALN-TRF);

- heating of the gyro-accelerometer center;

- introduction of navigation data into the computer by means of the NCP;

- introduction of initial true heading stored earlier (see STBY) or the initial true heading typed manually by means of the NCP keyboard during the alignment on stored heading.
  - NAV (navigation), which allows:
    - to start the equipment used in navigation (see ALN-TRF);
    - introduction of navigation data into the computer by means of NCP
    - to follow up the navigation according to the various navigation modes.

#### NAVIGATION INDICATOR (Figures 19 - 20)

#### 1 - SCOPE

The navigation indicator is an equipment which displays for the pilot, data generated by various navigation aid equipment.

The main functions of the instrument are to provide:

- the aircraft heading (true or gyromagnetic)
- a target position in relation to the aircraft
- the relative bearing of a radio compass beacon, of the radar antenna or of the target locked on by the missile homer
  - the heading deviation displayed for the autopilot
  - failure data related to the data displayed.

#### 2 - DESCRIPTION

#### A - Display

The indicator is provided with the following elements:

- a fixed index (2) representing the longitudinal axis of the aircraft;
- a graduated heading compass card (1) displaying the aircraft heading :
- gyromagnetic heading; this information is provided as a synchro signal by the interface electronics box or in emergency by the emergency gyromagnetic compass; a validity signal is associated with this synchro signal;
- true heading; this information is provided by the computer in digital form (ARINC link); the display of either one or the other hinges on the position of the associated selector (11);
  - a double pointer (3) displaying:
- the direct navigation course deviation  $\epsilon$ ; this is the deviation between the true course TC followed by the aircraft and the true course  $\theta$ t to be followed to re-acquire the navigation target whose number is displayed on the NCP ( $\theta$ t is equal to the target true bearing); the  $\theta$ t and TC data are provided by the computer in digital form (ARINC link);
- a single pointer (5) displaying the radio compass beacon relative bearing supplied by the ADF receiver or the radar antenna relative bearing supplied by the radar program box or the relative bearing of the target locked on by the missile homer, which information is supplied by the harmonization unit; a validity signal is associated with the radio compass relative bearing information; selection of this information is ensured by selector (10);
- a mobile index (6) displaying the heading to be followed by the aircraft in autopilot mode when in «displayed heading» function; the deviation between heading followed and heading to be followed is supplied by the autopilot;
- a four-digit distance counter (4) graduated in km or in 1/10 km; this displays the distance between aircraft and navigation target as supplied by the computer (ARINC link);

{263}------------------------------------------------

![](_page_263_Picture_0.jpeg)

MIRAGE F
MANUAL 12

- Four flags corresponding to failure of each information displayed, excepting for AP heading :

- flag (15): heading failure

flag (13) : double pointer failure
flag (12) : single pointer failure

- counter bar (14) : distance or computer failure.

<u>NOTE</u>: The flag indicates the invalidity of the associated information, irrespective of the fact that the error be of internal or external origin to the indicator.

#### B - Controls

The NAV IND comprises two controls:

- Heading display selector (11)

This control has two positions. Independently of the selected mode, it provides:

for position : the gyromagnetic heading

for position : the true heading.

- Single pointer display selector (10)

This display has three positions:

- «C»: corresponding to the display of the relative bearing of the radio compass beacon used;
- «R»: corresponding to the display of the radar antenna relative bearing;
- «M»: corresponding to the display of the relative bearing of the target locked on by the missile homer.

#### C - Signals provided by the NAV IND

The NAV IND provides the heading information to the data recorder acquisition unit (information provided by potentiometer).

#### 3 - OPERATION

#### A - General

The navigation indicator receives analog and digital data as also the reliability discrete signals.

The analog data are provided as synchro voltages used for some angular repetitions.

The data intended for the computation unit are received as digital messages.

These messages are controlled by specific reception circuits and are set to use by the indicator computation unit which then controls the associated display elements.

The reliability discrete signals are used for control of the various failure display flags.

The controls located on the front face of the indicator allow to send out display orders either to the angular repetition circuits or to the computation unit which uses and displays the data required.

- B Angular repetitions
- (1) Single pointer repetition circuit

This is a conventional repetition circuit.

This circuit comprises essentially a slaving system composed of the BCT2 synchro, an amplifier and a motor.

This slaving system is monitored by a monitoring circuit which takes also into account the validity signal associated with the repeated data.

(2) Displayed AP heading repetition circuit

This circuit comprises a slaving system similar to that of the single pointer repeat

- C Heading compass card slaving circuit
- (1) Gyromagnetic heading repetition

This circuit repeats the gyromagnetic heading as long as the NAV IND heading selector is positioned to this heading.

In these circumstances, heading data are provided as synchro voltages. The matching system allows to convert these voltages into usable data for the slaving circuits of the card.

The  $\pm$  28 V reliability discrete signal allows the heading failure flag to disappear, through a heading monitoring circuit.

(2) True heading repetition

When the heading selector is set to true heading position, the heading data in digital form is checked and validated prior to being transmitted to the computation unit which will put it to use for positioning of the true heading compass card.

In this eventuality, the discrete signal for disappearance of the heading failure flag is provided by the computation unit.

(3) Heading repetition

The heading repetition slaving motor drives a repetition potentiometer at the same time as the compass card.

- D Double pointer slaving circuit
- (1)  $\theta t$  TC repetition (NAV mode)

In this case, it is ensured by two slaving systems which receive their data from the computation unit.

The first slaving system  $\theta$  repeats the data  $\theta t$ .

The second slaving system repeats the true course data.

A mechanical differential performs the operation  $\theta$  - TC and positions the double pointer in direct navigation course deviation  $\epsilon$ .

The  $\theta$ t data is received by the computation unit in digital form only.

Since the validity of this data is checked at input, the monitoring of the computation unit itself will detect the processing or display shortcomings.

Any possible failings are indicated by the double pointer flag.

#### E - Distance counter control

The control of the distance counter wheels is solely ensured by the computation unit which receives the distance data.

Distance data, its processing and display are subject to monitoring which, in case of fault, causes the distance counter bar to appear.

#### F - Digital input circuit

NAV reception circuit

This is a coupling and decoding circuit which sets navigation data into suitable digital form.

These data are received in the form of a serial digital message comprising a parity bit and a sign + validity bit.

The message is checked upon reception and its possibly poor conformation will be cause for indication of an associated failure.

#### 4 - POWER SUPPLY

#### A - 115 V/400 Hz power supply

It is provided through the aircraft 115 V/400 Hz AC system 1 in normal operation or through the 115 V/400 Hz emergency system in case of failure of system 1. Switching-over from one system to the other is automatic.

Through the aircraft power system, the internal power supply module of the indicator generates all voltages required for reliable performance of the instrument.

#### These voltages are:

- + 5 V logic required for supply of logic circuits
- - 5 V logic required for supply of memories and computation unit
- + 5 V and 5 V for slaving modules
- - 12 V for slaving modules
- + 12 V required for distance counter wheel control.

It serves as a reference for demodulation of synchro analog data (MH, single pointer relative bearing, AP heading deviation).

C - Ancillaries + 28 V power supply

This voltage is provided by the aircraft DC system 1 and serves to control the failure flags.

D - Lighting 5 V/400 Hz power supply

The lighting voltage is provided by the lighting distributor, controlled itself by the lighting control box.

E - System 2 + 28 V power supply

This voltage is not a true power supply but a forcing of the heading slaving for repetition of the GMH in case of alternator failure, even if the NAV IND heading selector is set to true heading position.
