### NAVIGATION OUTPUT MULTIPLIER (Figure 22)

Restricted

#### 1 - PARAMETERS SUPPLIED TO RADAR

Through the navigation output multiplier, the radar system receives the following data supplied in analog form :

- height of aircraft with respect to target h target
- oblique distance between aircraft and target  $\rho$  obl
- course deviation sin and  $\cos \theta$ -TC
- horizontal ground speed GSH
- antenna relative bearing plus drift sin and cos BRG ant + Dr (synchro)
- target relative bearing
- target elevation.

#### 2 - PARAMETERS SUPPLIED TO RECONNAISSANCE SYSTEM

The reconnaissance system receives the following data:

- aircraft latitude LA
- aircraft longitude GA
- true heading TH
- roll R
- radio altimeter height h
- altitude H
- horizontal ground speed GSH
- horizontal ground speed/radio altimeter height GSH/h

It also receives the navigation computer and navigation output multiplier reliability signals.

#### 3 - OPERATION OF NAVIGATION OUTPUT MULTIPLIER

It receives the parameters in serial digital form. Each datum (serial word) includes an address (identification of datum) and an quantity part.

It distributes the data in analog form (voltages).

After reception, the datum is transmitted to an input register.

The address is decoded if the monitoring system authorizes it.

The quantity is sent to the digital-to-analog converter.

The decoder orients the selected output channel to the corresponding datum contained in the converter.

The output channels include an analog store followed or not by an output multiplier.

The output to the reconnaissance system is in the serial digital form.

#### DOPPLER RADAR SYSTEM

#### 1 — <u>SCOPE</u> (figure 25)

The Doppler radar system calculates the aircraft ground speed by using the Doppler effect: it measures the difference between the frequency of an electromagnetic wave beamed at the ground and the frequency of the same wave received after reflection.

The ground speed is used for long-term correction of the speed calculated by the accelerometers of the gyro-accelerometer system, and for the updating and stabilizing of this speed.

The system comprises:

- Doppler radar 34S
- main computer 104F
- mode and heading selector panel 37F
- navigation control panel 9F.

The system is supplied with 115 V/400 Hz by system 2.

#### 2 - PRINCIPLE

A - Outline of principle of Doppler-Fizeau effect

Assume a wave of frequency FO emitted by a transmitter. It travels at a velocity C (velocity of electromagnetic waves: 3.108 m/s).

The fixed transmitter emits, during time interval t, N number of alternations. A fixed receiver receives during time interval t, the same number N of alternations. The frequency detected by the receiver is equal to FO.

Let us assume that the transmitter is moving towards the receiver at a speed S. The receiver will receive during the given time period a number of alternations N1, greater than N; the frequency detected by the receiver is higher than FO. If the transmitter is moving away from the receiver, the same principle applies, but with the reverse effect, i.e. the frequency received is less than FO.

Now let us assume that an object moving at a speed GS emits a wave of frequency FO, and that this wave is received by the same object after being reflected by a fixed obstacle located in the path of the emission.

The previously mentioned considerations apply, i.e. the frequency F' of the wave received by the obstacle is different from FO (greater than FO if the object is moving towards the obstacle, and less than FO if it is moving away from the obstacle). The frequency of the wave reflected by the obstacle is F'. Allowing for its speed, the object receives this wave at a frequency F".

It can be seen, that there are two successive alterations in the frequency of a wave emitted by an object and received by the same object following reflection from a fixed obstacle: the frequency alters from FO to F' and then from F' to F". The difference between frequencies FO and F", which is a function of speed, is called the Doppler frequency (DF).

#### B - Calculation of longitudinal ground speed DSX

The above conclusions may be applied to an aircraft in horizontal flight.

A transmitter-receiver in the aircraft emits an electromagnetic wave in the form of a conical pencil-beam whose axis is in the symmetry plane of the aircraft. The wave is beamed forwards and produces an angle  $\alpha$  with the horizontal fuselage reference line.

{275}------------------------------------------------

### MIRAGE F

MANUAL 12

![](_page_275_Figure_3.jpeg)

Restricted

FIGURE 25 - BLOCK DIAGRAM OF DOPPLER RADAR SYSTEM

{276}------------------------------------------------

![](_page_276_Picture_2.jpeg)

![](_page_276_Picture_4.jpeg)

Because of the relative speed of the transmitter-receiver with regard to the ground, the wave which is received at the aircraft after reflection from the ground, has been subjected to changes in frequency due to the Doppler effect. Considering the relation which exists between Doppler frequency and the speed at which the transmitter-receiver is moving, it is possible to calculate the speed once the Doppler frequency has been measured.

It should be noted that the speed thus obtained is in fact the component of the aircraft speed along the axis of the beam (DSX)A. An adjustment based on angle  $\alpha$  permits the calculation of the aircraft speed DSX.

In horizontal flight, with no wind and at constant speed, DF is constant provided that the longitudinal attitude of the aircraft is constant, since the axis of the beam is fixed in relation to the aircraft. Any change in longitudinal attitude causes a variation between the inclination of the beam and the horizontal. This in turn modifies the component of DSX according to the axis of the beam, and again, alters DF (for a similar DSX value). This is interpreted as a variation in aircraft speed.

To correct this error, a pencil-beam is emitted rearwards from the aircraft with the same inclination from the fuselage horizontal reference line. For this rear beam, the Doppler frequency variation, resulting from aircraft pitch is the inverse of the Doppler frequency variation of the forward beam. Using this characteristic, it is possible to obtain a DSX practically insusceptible to pitch variations.

#### C - Calculation of transverse ground speed DSY

The above principle applies to calculation of the transverse ground speed: an electromagnetic wave is emitted in the form of a conical pencil-beam A inclined from the symmetry plane of the aircraft at an angle  $\beta$ .

The Doppler frequency measured is a function of the projection of speed DSY in the direction of the beam.

A second beam B is emitted, similar to the first in relation to the aircraft symmetry plane. For this second beam, the Doppler frequency variation resulting from aircraft pitch is the inverse of the Doppler frequency variation of the first beam. In the same way as for the calculation of longitudinal speed, this characteristic is used to obtain a value for transverse speed practically insusceptible to variations produced by aircraft roll.

![](_page_276_Picture_13.jpeg)

{277}------------------------------------------------

![](_page_277_Picture_2.jpeg)

#### D - Calculation of ground speed components

#### (1) Orientation of beams

Optimum orientation of the beams allows their number to be kept to three; two beams A and B are directed forwards, symmetrically placed in relation to the symmetry plane of the aircraft, and inclined from it at an angle  $\beta$ . The plane of these beams forms an angle  $\alpha$  with the fuselage horizontal reference line.

For technical simplicity in the antenna, the rearward-pointing beam C has the same inclination in relation to the aircraft symmetry plane as beam B.

![](_page_277_Picture_7.jpeg)

(2) Effect of ground speed components on Doppler frequency relative to each beam (figure 26).

The Doppler radar emits, then, three electromagnetic beams - A, B and C. It receives the signals reflected by the ground, each signal following the axis of its beam - A, B or C.

The longitudinal ground speed DSX has an effect upon beams A and B, in that the frequency of reception is offset in comparison with the frequency of emission. The difference in frequency corresponds to Doppler frequency DFX. It is identical for the paths of both beams, since the components of DSX, depending on the axes of the two beams (DSX)A and (DSX)B, are identical. Equally the longitudinal ground speed DSX acts on beam C, in that its frequency of reception is offset in comparison with the frequency of emission. This difference is equal to — DFX since the DSX component along the axis of beam C, (DSX)C, is equal and opposite in sign to the DSX component along beams A and B.

The transverse ground speed DSY acts on beam A, in that its frequency of reception is offset in comparison with the frequency of emission. This difference corresponds to Doppler frequency DFY. This speed DSY also has the effect of offsetting the frequencies of reception of beams B and C in relation to their frequencies of emission. The frequency difference is equal to - DFY, since the DSY components along the axes of beams B and C - (DSY)B and (DSY)C - are equal and opposite in sign to the DSY component of beam A (DSY)A.

The speed along axis Z, (DSZ), has the effect of offsetting the frequencies of reception of beams A, B and C in comparison with their relative frequencies of emission. This frequency difference corresponds to Doppler frequency DFZ. It is identical for the paths of all three beams, since the DFZ components along the axes of beams A, B and C = (DSZ)A, (DSZ)B and (DSZ)C = are equal.

{278}------------------------------------------------

![](_page_278_Figure_3.jpeg)

![](_page_278_Figure_4.jpeg)

![](_page_278_Figure_5.jpeg)

![](_page_278_Figure_6.jpeg)

FIGURE 26 – EFFECT OF GROUND SPEED COMPONENTS ON DOPPLER FREQUENCY

{279}------------------------------------------------

![](_page_279_Picture_0.jpeg)

![](_page_279_Picture_1.jpeg)

AVIONS MARCEL DASSAULT BREGUET AVIATION . Restricted

MANUAL 12

The components of the ground speed are DSX, DSY and DSZ; therefore, for each beam, the ground speed will give rise to differences between the frequency at which each wave is emitted and the frequency at which it is received after reflection from the ground. For each wave, the difference in frequency («Doppler frequency») is equal to the sum of the differences caused by each speed component, i.e.

- for beam A: DFA = DFX + DFY + DFZ.

for beam B : DFB = DFX + DFY + DFZ.

- for beam C : DFC = DFX + DFY + DFZ.

The Doppler frequencies for the various beams are obtained by comparison of the frequencies of reception with those of emission. From these, the system calculates the three components of the aircraft speed — DSX, DSY and DSZ.

#### (3) Frequency spectrum

The beam emitted by the antenna is not of extreme fineness; rather, it has a certain width, which can be considered as a composite of individual, infinitely fine beams. Each of these individual beams has its own Doppler frequency depending on its inclination from the horizontal. Thus the energy received is distributed over a frequency spectrum.

In practice, the dispersion of Doppler frequencies resulting from this width of the beam gives the Doppler spectrum received from a normal ground a symmetrical aspect centered on a preferred frequency DF. This preferred frequency can be considered as being generated by the individual beam of inclination  $\alpha 1$  which practically merges with the mean axis of the beam. This value  $\alpha 1$  is known to the manufacturer of the equipment. Working from this known preferred frequency, it is therefore possible to determine the speed component of the aircraft along the axis along the is and from this, to calculate the aircraft speed. For a given aircraft speed, this preferred frequency DF is not exactly the same over water, since water will reflect a greater part of the energy along an angle  $\alpha 2$ , greater than  $\alpha 1$ . This value a2 is also known to the manufacturer of the equipment. From this measured frequency, it is therefore possible to obtain, in the same way as that already mentioned, the speed component of the aircraft along the axis  $\alpha 2$  (and not  $\alpha 1$ ) and from this, to calculate the aircraft speed (after a correction based on  $\alpha 2$ ).

It can be seen, therefore, that the formula which allows the calculation of the aircraft speed by measuring the preferred Doppler frequency must not be the same for an aircraft flying over water as for an aircraft flying over land. The changeover from one to the other is carried out manually using the «Land-Sea» control.

![](_page_279_Picture_13.jpeg)

{280}------------------------------------------------

# AVIONS MARCEL DASSAULT BREGUET AVIATION

#### Restricted

![](_page_280_Picture_2.jpeg)

#### 3 - FUNCTIONAL DESCRIPTION

#### A - Antennas

The antennas consist of two radiating beams situated side by side; one is used for emitting, the other for receiving. The emitting antenna produces three beams defining the three directions in which Doppler effect is to be measured. The receiving antenna receives the electromagnetic signals reflected by the ground for the respective beams.

Emission and reception take place on a continuous basis - continuous wave emission (CW principle).

#### B - Transmitter

The transmitter is composed of two microwave sources.

One source provides frequency F01 and delivers a continuous supply for the emission of the two forward beams. It also serves as a local oscillator for the rear beam receiver.

The second source provides frequency F02 and delivers a continuous supply for the emission of the rear beam. It also acts as a local oscillator for the front beam receivers.

#### C - Receivers

Each measurement beam has an associated super-heterodyne-type reception channel. Each channel comprises a band-pass microwave filter for protection against local jammers, and a mixer followed by an amplifier centered on the frequency difference of the solid sources, F1 = F01 - F02. The receiver gain is controlled by the level of the signal tapped after detection of the Doppler signal (AGC).

The Doppler signal contained in the receiver output signal is transposed around a stable frequency fo. The transposing circuit receives a signal F01 - F02. This enables a Doppler spectrum independent of frequency changes at the sources to be provided, with fo as its center.

#### D - Search - tracking

#### (1) Scope - principle

Each channel contains a «search-tracking» circuit.

For each channel, the purpose of this circuit is to provide a signal which is representative of the Doppler frequency of the associated beam.

The circuit has three operating modes : search, tracking, memory.

Search consists in adjusting the frequency of a voltage-controlled oscillator (VCO) to a frequency which corresponds to that of the signal delivered by the transposing circuit.

A recopy of the transposing circuit output signal is thus obtained.

Tracking consists in maintaining this value irrespective of how the Doppler signal changes in time. The frequency of the VCO thus determines the Doppler frequency (transposed around fo). This recopying of the transposing circuit output signal enables the frequency of the VCO to be aligned with the center frequency of the Doppler spectrum. It is thus possible to obtain a precise Doppler frequency.

In memory mode, the VCO frequency is adjusted from external speed information. This mode is selected when the Doppler signal cannot be used. Adjustment of the VCO frequency enables the supply of representative Doppler information (VCO frequency) to be readily renewed once the Doppler signal is able to be used again.

. . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .

{281}------------------------------------------------

MIRAGE F
MANUAL 12

#### (2) Circumstances for changing the operating modes

The search-tracking circuit will change from tracking mode to memory mode when one or other of the following situations is encountered:

- the quality of the signal received by one or other of the three channels is insufficient (signal/noise ratio too low);
- during a roll, the aircraft attitude causes the Doppler radar to exceed its measurement limits; this information is furnished by the main computer which transmits a «memory forcing» order when the angle of roll is greater than 30°;
  - an onboard jammer is operating: this information is provided by the main computer;
- a vertical parasite is detected on any of the three beams (see paragraph 5 Monitoring of vertical return).

Following a period of operation in memory mode, switching to search mode is permitted if the main computer estimates that the Doppler radar is operating within attitude limits that would enable operation in tracking mode (search enabling information), or if the jammer is no longer operating.

When operation in memory mode has been imposed following detection of a Doppler signal of insufficient quality, or following detection of a vertical parasite, switching to search mode is automatic after 30 seconds.

#### (3) Operation in tracking mode

The Doppler frequency information is obtained through a slaved system.

The operation of this system is schematized as follows:

![](_page_281_Picture_14.jpeg)

The frequency of a VCO is set by its control voltage. In the tracking window (narrow band filter), the oscillator output signal is compared with the Doppler signal (delivered by transposing circuit). The tracking window is linked to a discriminator and delivers to the integrator a voltage whose sign and amplitude depend on the direction and the value of the frequency gap between Doppler signal and oscillator signal.

The output voltage of the integrator is delivered to the VCO, in such a way that the frequency gap between the signals compared in the tracking window tends to cancel itself.

{282}------------------------------------------------

![](_page_282_Picture_0.jpeg)

MIRAGE F

MANUAL 12

In equilibrium (slaving achieved), the oscillator frequency is identical to the center frequency of the Doppler spectrum (transposing circuit output signal) and the input voltage of the integrator is nil. Thus the oscillator control voltage (integrator output voltage) represents the Doppler frequency. From now on, the voltage-controlled oscillator constantly follows the variations of the Doppler signal, and the radar is locked on ; it is now operating in «tracking» mode. This is the normal operating mode for the radar, enabled by the aircraft attitude, absence of parasites or jamming and the quality of the signals received.

«Search» consists in scanning the frequencies with the VCO until lock-on with Doppler signal is achieved.

#### (4) Memory mode - Prepositioning of tracking windows

When operating in memory mode, the VCO frequency of each channel is adjusted using external speed information. This is achieved taking into account the following principles:

- the frequency of the voltage-controlled oscillator represents a speed. From the three VCO frequencies (channels A, B and C), a signal is generated which is representative of a ground speed. This speed is compared with the «hybrid» speed calculated from accelerations provided by the gyro-accelerometer center; the comparison is carried out in the main computer. The result (speed difference - Doppler realignment) is fed to the three search-tracking integrators which then modify their VCO frequencies until the difference is reduced to nil. The information generated by the Doppler signals has now been slaved to the hybrid speed. The tracking windows are said to be «prepositioned».

#### (5) Monitoring of vertical return

When the aircraft is flying over a stretch of calm water, a parasitic «vertical return» signal is received, whose Doppler frequency corresponds to the vertical speed of the aircraft. To eliminate interference from «Spurious lock-on» with this parasitic signal, the method used is to detect it in a window which covers the range of Doppler frequencies corresponding to the vertical speed of the aircraft. The setting of this window is relative to the vertical speed of the aircraft, determined by external information.

Presence of this parasitic signal in any of the three channels will cause switch-over to memory mode to avoid bringing the tracking windows onto the parasitic signal, should the signal proper disappear.

#### E - Processing of Doppler frequency information

The voltage-controlled oscillator of each channel supplies the Doppler frequency transposed about fo, to the main computer via a digital interface circuit. Provided that the Doppler radar system is neither in memory nor in search mode, the main computer determines the longitudinal and transverse speeds of the aircraft.

The law which enables calculation of speed using frequency information is modified according to the position of the «LAND - SEA» selector.

If the search-tracking circuits are operating in memory mode, the main computer takes the information received by the Doppler radar system and superimposes it on the speed value obtained from the gyro-accelerometer center (prepositioning of tracking windows).

{283}------------------------------------------------

### MIRAGE F

#### Restricted

MANUAL 12

#### F - Test initiation

AVIONS MARCEL DASSA

The doppler radar system can be qualitatively checked for operation and connection to the main computer by use of a test device.

The test consists in generating a Doppler signal which is then applied to the inputs of the receivers of each channel; therefore the test covers the entire radar system except for the antennas.

The test is initiated on the ground, with the MHSP mode selector set to TEST, using the main computer. The main computer checks the signal from the Doppler radar for conformity with the theoretical value of the Doppler test signals.

During the whole test, the «MDPL» light on the NCP is on.

In the event of malfunction, this light will flash, and will go out when operation becomes normal.

G - Signals exchanged between Doppler radar and main computer.

Via the Digibus, the Doppler radar supplies the main computer with the following information :

- Doppler radar status (radar working, operation in memory or search mode, presence of vertical parasite).
  - Doppler frequencies transposed about fo : FA = DFA + fo, FB = DFB + fo, FC = DFC + fo,
- variation of fo (difference between transposing frequency and its nominal value) memorized in main computer.

Via the Digibus the main computer supplies the Doppler radar with the following information:

- speed difference along the three beams and the corresponding validity,
- jammer in operation,
- memory forcing,
- test in progress,
- search enabling,
- vertical velocity VV.

### PRINCIPLE OF RADIO-ALTIMETER SYSTEM (Figure 1)

The radio-altimeter system is intended to measure the exact height between the aircraft wheels and the overflown ground, independently of the atmospheric pressure.

The system includes:

- transmitter-receiver 102R
- transmission antenna 104R
- reception antenna 105R
- ECM filter 165R
- diode-resistor box 114R
- radio-altimeter test relay 97R.

The aircraft height is displayed on the head-up display.

Besides, the radio-altimeter system is connected to :

- the autopilot (not used),
- the main computer which receives the height and a reliability signal
- the symbol generator box which receives the height and displays it as well as the reliability signal,
  - the radar scope to warn the pilot when the preset height is reached.

The transmitter-receiver generates and transmits towards the ground a wave (frequency-modulated by a sawtooth signal) through the transmission antenna.

It receives this wave reflected by the ground through the reception antenna and, after mixing the received signal with a fraction of the signal transmitted at the same time, it deduces the beat signal from which it determines the aircraft height above ground level.

The radio-altimeter system is equipped with a monitoring system and a self-test system.

{296}------------------------------------------------

### OPERATION OF RADIO-ALTIMETER SYSTEM (Figure 4)

#### 1 - TRANSMITTER-RECEIVER

#### A - Principle

A wave frequency modulated by a sawtooth signal is transmitted to ground. At time t1, the wave frequency is f1. At time t2, after being reflected by ground, the wave is received with the same frequency f1.

At this time t2, the transmission frequency has become f2.

In a mixer, the received signal, with frequency f1, is multiplied by a fraction of the transmission signal, with frequency f2.

The beating signal with the following frequency is then collected:

$$fb = f2 - f1$$

![](_page_296_Figure_11.jpeg)

Let us call  $\mathcal C$  the time interval t2-t1: this is the duration of the two-way travel of the wave between the aircraft and ground. It is called «transition time».

Applying basic relations between similar triangles provides:

$$\frac{fb}{\Delta f} = \frac{\mathcal{C}}{T}$$

where  $\Delta f$  is the fixed frequency deviation in the radio-altimeter.

On the other hand:

$$\mathcal{C} = \frac{2Hra}{C}$$

where Hra is the aircraft height and C the radio-electrical wave speed.

Consequently:

Hra = 
$$\frac{C}{2} \frac{T}{\Delta f}$$
 fb

{297}------------------------------------------------

### MIRAGE F

MANUAL 12

AVIONS MARCEL DASSAULT RESTRICTED RESTRICTED

In the radio-altimeter, fb is kept constant by slaving T to height h, and so T provides the height information.

This measurement is made by period-meters performing the signal width/DC voltage conversion.

#### B - Operation

The radio-altimeter transceiver consists of a servo loop and several components external to the loop.

#### (1) Servo loop

The servo loop includes:

- one UHF source (modulator-transmitter) supplying the transmission signal; this signal is frequency modulated by a sawtooth signal from a sawtooth signal generator; the constant frequency deviation is set to  $\Delta f = 123$  MHz;
- one mixer generating beating signal fb from the transmission signal reflected by ground and a fraction of the signal transmitted at the same time;
- one audio frequency amplifier receiving beating signal fb, with the gain automatically matching the height;
- one tracking discriminator receiving the amplified beating signal and comparing it to its own frequency fo of 100 kHz; when fb is not strictly equal to fo, the circuit delivers an error voltage  $\epsilon$  p;
  - one integrator receiving error voltage  $\epsilon$  p ;
  - one dynamic gain corrector stabilizing the loop regardless of the operating height;
- one sawtooth signal generator controlled by the output voltage from the gain corrector and controlling the modulator frequency.

#### (2) Components external to servo loop

These are listed below.

- One contrast discriminator, with the positive peak centered on fo. This discriminator is also driven by the output of the audio frequency amplifier.

The discriminator detects the presence of a beating signal near frequency fo. If this signal disappears, or if its level is insufficient, the output DC voltage from this discriminator drops below a preset value called «contrast threshold».

- A series of «search/tracking logic» circuits, controlled by the contrast discriminator output; when the beating signal disappears or decreases, these circuits trigger an automatic signal search process, open the servo loop (gate), supply a warning information and suppress the height information. On acquisition of a beating signal of suitable level and frequency, the output voltage from the contrast discriminator becomes higher than the contrast threshold, and the search/tracking logic stops the search phase, closes the servo loop, authorizes the height information and cancels the warning information.

This is the normal radio-altimeter operation or «tracking phase».

- One semi-logarithmic period-meter performing the sawtooth width/DC voltage conversion.

{298}------------------------------------------------

{299}------------------------------------------------

MIRAGE F MANUAL 12

- two linear period-meters.

- a device comparing the A/C actual height information from the semi-logarithmic period-meter, with the preset height information.

According to the result obtained, a logic may or may not trigger the illumination of the height indicator lights.

![](_page_299_Figure_6.jpeg)

{300}------------------------------------------------

. . 

{301}------------------------------------------------

![](_page_301_Figure_0.jpeg)

{302}------------------------------------------------

![](_page_302_Picture_0.jpeg)

MIRAGE F

MANUAL 12

#### 2 - HEIGHT DISPLAY

#### A - Height repeating circuit

The signal from the linear periodic-meter No. 1 is used by the symbol generator box displaying the height scale in the HUD sight.

#### B - Preset safe height

The purpose of this presetting is to enable the pilot to fly the aircraft above a safe height by signalling, through illumination of the red light, that the aircraft reaches or is below this height.

The safe height selection control, located on the HUD sight, drives the needle of a potentiometer the output voltage of which, proportional to the preset height, is applied to a control logic of the transceiver which also receives the matched semi-log voltage representing the actual aircraft height. After comparison of both voltages, a control circuit acts on a relay which illuminates or extinguishes the safe height indicator lights.

#### 3 - MONITORING

Only the radio altimeter transceiver is monitored. Monitoring is performed on the transmission-reception loop, at the contrast discriminator.

When the beat signal disappears or becomes lower than the contrast threshold, the search/tracking logic triggers the search process by opening the transmission-reception loop, and controls storage of the output voltages from the period-meters.

If the beat signal does not return to a suitable level, the warning circuit cuts off the signal controlling the radio altimeter correct operation relay; this causes:

- suppression of the voltage which
  - cuts in the control logic of the height indicator lights on the radar scope (the lights go out)
- transmits to the main computer and to the symbol generator box the radio altimeter correct operation information.

#### 4 - POWER SUPPLY

The radio altimeter transceiver is fed with:

- 115V/400 Hz through AC system 1, phase B
- 28 V DC through DC system 1.

Built-in power supply units generate the voltages required for operation of the transceiver, from the 115 V/400 Hz power supply.

{303}------------------------------------------------

![](_page_303_Picture_0.jpeg)

MIRAGE F
MANUAL 12

#### Transceiver

A starting circuit is supplied with 115 V/400 Hz as soon as the aircraft is energized.

It is controlled by the radio altimeter operation relay when this relay is energized, and permits switching of the starting relay to active position. The latter supplies a power supply unit which delivers the voltages required for operation of the transceiver.

The hour meter is also supplied with 115 V/400 Hz power.

The 28 V DC voltage from the aircraft system is distributed :

- to the common contact of the radio altimeter reliability relay for use by the main computer and the symbol generator box,

- to the control logic of safety height lights after the lighting distributor relay has passed through

deenergized position.

This voltage is also distributed to the safety height lights circuit through the test relay, and to the coil of the test prevention relay.

#### 5 - SELF-TEST

Pressing the test control on the head-up display causes the energization of the test relay which, through one of its active contacts, applies a ground to the transceiver test logic.

#### As a result:

- the AF channel is cut off, which causes the transmission-reception loop to open and the height counter reticle to appear on the head-up display;
  - the search is cancelled;
- a calibrated current is applied to the input of the dynamic gain corrector so as to obtain a frequency corresponding to a O foot height at the output of the sawtooth signal generator.

### PRINCIPLE OF RADIO-COMPASS SYSTEM (Figure 1)

The radio-compass system is a radio-navigational facility operating with a ground beacon and supplying the pilot with the beacon bearing.

The system is composed of the following:

- radio-compass receiver 124R
- radio-compass control unit 123R
- loop antenna 130R
- quadrantal error corrector 129R
- sense antenna 128R
- antenna coupler 127R
- coaxial adapter 126R
- 26 V/400 Hz computing voltage filter 125R
- 28 V DC supply voltage filter 122R
- a display unit: navigation indicator 68F for reading the radio-compass beacon bearing
- radio selector box 29R which is supplied with the AF signals detected by the receiver (beacon identification and modulation).

The receiver can operate (according to selection made on control unit):

- automatically (ADF mode): tracking and sensing take place automatically
- as a traffic receiver (ANT mode).

The system is provided with a controlled test system.

### OPERATION OF RADIO-COMPASS SYSTEM (Figures 4, 5 and 6)

#### 1 - PRINCIPLE OF OPERATION

The method for finding the transmitting station direction is based upon the «radio direction-finding» principle.

The loop antenna consists of two stationary loops located in vertical and perpendicular planes. These two loops respectively feed power to two perpendicular-axis windings of a «radio direction-finder». The longitudinal and transverse components of the magnetic field radiated by the loop antenna are thus transmitted to the «radio direction-finder», in which the direction of the magnetic field is restored.

A winding able to turn about the reproduced field just acts as a rotating aerial. Each time the winding is set turning, the induced voltage goes through two zero values. These two zero values are such that the rotor winding axis is located at  $90^{\circ}$  from the magnetic field direction; therefore this axis follows the direction of the transmitting station on ground (since the component of a transmitted wave magnetic field is perpendicular to the propagation direction).

If the winding of the radio direction-finder rotor is not lined up with one of the two «zero» directions, an induced voltage is generated and amplified, then this voltage controls a motor which returns the rotor to a zero voltage position (rotor slaving to the zero voltage position - radio direction-finding principle).

#### 2 - SENSING

#### A - Principle

Now that the two zero detection positions can be obtained, it is necessary to remove the uncertainty margin of  $180^{\circ}$  in the indication of the transmitter direction; this is done using the «sensing» system.

To this end, the amplification and computation circuits make one of the two loop positions unsteady. In other words, assuming that both positions are possible, the least modification in the direction of the transmitting station will cause the loop in «unsteady» position, through the computation circuits, to tilt by 180° and resume its «steady» position.

Figure 4 shows the rotation control principle applicable to the direction-finder loop, from the two possible «zero detection» positions.

#### B - Circuit operation

(a) The diagrams in figure 4 show that, if the direction of rotation of the loop is determined by the direction in which the loop signal is phase-shifted with respect to the sensing signal, only one of the two zero detection positions is possible. Therefore, the computing circuits are intended to determine the direction of phase-shift.

It is a priori assumed that the equipment is constructed so that the loop signal has a phase lead which respect to the antenna signal, the electromagnetic finder will rotate counterclockwise, and vice versa.

{317}------------------------------------------------

![](_page_317_Figure_0.jpeg)

NOTE: So as to differentiate the direction in which the lines of force of the magnetic field pass through the direction-finder, an index integral with the loop is shown.

{318}------------------------------------------------

![](_page_318_Figure_3.jpeg)

{319}------------------------------------------------

![](_page_319_Picture_0.jpeg)

MIRAGE F MANUAL 12

(b) Determining the phase-shift direction of «loop» signal with respect to «sense» signal.

The «loop» and «sense» signals are phase-shifted by 90° one with respect to the other. A mixture of these two signals will result in a periodic signal with a phase value comprised between those of the two original signals.

As a matter of fact, for a «sense» signal lagging by 90° with respect to the «loop» signal, the signal resulting from their mixture will have a phase lead with respect to the «sense» signal. If, by some means or other, the «loop» signal is inverted (180° phase shift), the «sense» signal will be leading by 90° with respect to the new «loop» signal, and the resulting mixture will have a phase lag with respect to the «sense» signal.

Therefore by periodically inverting the «loop» signal, one obtains a «mixture» signal with a phase periodically varying in an abrupt way, according to the rhythm of the inversion command (i.e. a phase-modulated signal).

Such inversion is controlled by a 90 Hz oscillator : the positive half-wave enables the «loop» signal to be transmitted without modification, whereas the negative half-wave permits its inversion.

As shown by the diagrams of figure 5, in a phase detector, it is enough to take as a phase reference the «mixture» signal when the 90 Hz signal is in its positive half-wave, and to measure the sign of the phase-shift undergone by this «mixture» signal when the 90 Hz signal is in its negative half-wave.

If the «mixture» signal undergoes a phase lag, this means that the «loop» signal has a lead with respect to the «sense» signal.

On the contrary, if it undergoes a phase lead, this means that the «loop» signal has a lag with respect to the «sense» signal.

The phase detector output is then function of the phase-shift direction of the «loop» signal with respect to the «sense» signal. Therefore it is the phase detector which will determine, through a motor, the rotation direction of the loop.

#### (c) Remarks

- For easier understanding, the values of the various frequency scales have not been respected on the diagrams of figure 5.

- Before reaching the phase detector, the «mixture» signal is subject to various frequency modi-

fications.

- The amplitude modulation of the received signal is extracted to supply the audio identification signal.

- The phase-shift undergone by the «mixture» signal is function of the «loop» signal amplitude.

#### 3 - RECEPTION CIRCUIT TUNING

The «frequency selected» signal is transmitted to the ADF control unit in the binary form.

The base of the tuning circuit consists of a single-frequency synthesizer crystal and of an automatic band selector (solid-state circuits).

#### 4 - IDENTIFICATION

For identifying the selected transmitting station, various procedures can be used, depending on how the carrier wave is modulated.

{320}------------------------------------------------

{321}------------------------------------------------

![](_page_321_Figure_0.jpeg)

{322}------------------------------------------------

![](_page_322_Picture_0.jpeg)

MIRAGE F MANUAL 12

#### ս - Sense antenna adaptation

The radio-compass receiver will operate correctly provided that a 3000 pF capacitance is inserted in the coaxial cable/sense antenna assembly, at the input of the receiver.

This is obtained by inserting a coaxial adaptor and an antenna coupler in the antenna circuit.

#### C - Remark

The electrical signals fed by the loop and sense antennas are phase-shifted by  $90^{\circ}$  one with respect to the other. As a matter of fact, the signal fed by the sense antenna is proportional to the electric field. On the other hand, the signal fed by the loop antenna is proportional to the magnetic field derivative. This induced voltage is therefore phase-shifted by  $90^{\circ}$  with respect to the magnetic field and consequently with respect to the electric field and to the voltage fed by the sense antenna.

#### BEARING INDICATION

The rotor of a synchro-transmitter is integral with the loop of the direction-finder. Therefore, mis rotor, which is fed with 26 V/400 Hz power, will indicate the setting position of the loop with respect to the A/C centerline. This information is transmitted to the synchro-receiver of the navigation indicator through the ADF/pole setting and ADF/radar switching relays.

#### 8 - AF LOCKING AND HORIZONTAL POSITIONING

The control logic circuit produces an AF circuit locking pulse, as well as a signal used for slaving the direction-finder loop and consequently the navigation indicator pointer at 90°, in one of the following cases: switching of tuning frequency band, no phase-locking of the synthesizer or loss of signal (no AGC).

# AVIONS MARCEL DASSAULT BREGUET AVIATION

#### Restricted

![](_page_323_Picture_2.jpeg)

#### A - Operation in ANT mode

In this mode, the radio-compass operates as a radio wave receiver only.

The control logic then achieves the «loop» signal disconnection. Only the «sense» antenna signal is used to supply an audio output.

During this time, a circuit slaves the loop at 90°.

#### B - Unmodulated continuous wave reception

To provide for the identification of a station transmitting unmodulated continuous wave signals a 1020 Hz oscillator modulates the amplitude of the last intermediate frequency signal.

#### 5 - TEST CIRCUITS

The test consists in generating two signals representing the direction of a dummy beacon, and to apply these signals:

- to the windings of the direction-finder stator,
- to the input of the sense signal amplifier.

The test signals are generated from the frequency supplied by the synthesizer, which frequency is mixed-up with a signal delivered by a test oscillator. The frequency obtained corresponds to the frequency displayed on the control unit.

The signal processing and distribution take place in the same manner as in manual operation (ADF mode). The test result is obtained in the form of a determined bearing value.

In addition, the AF oscillator is triggered and permits the test of the AF section.

The test is controlled either from the control unit or directly on the receiver.

NOTE: During the test, the sense antenna is connected to the ground.

#### 6 - ANTENNAS

#### A - Loop antenna compensation

The electromagnetic field is distorted by the aircraft in such a manner that the direction from which the signal is received is shifted towards the nose within the first and fourth sectors, and towards the tail within the second and third sectors.

This is remedied by simply applying a given correction to the «direction» indication transmitted by the loop antenna. As the antenna sees the transmitting station offset towards the A/C centerline, it suffices, for the transmission of the two voltages representing the longitudinal and transversal components of the transmission propagation direction, to reduce the voltage representing the component along the A/C centerline. This is achieved by adjusting the alignment of the loop antenna with respect to the A/C centerline.

A quadrantal error corrector corrects the error due to loop antenna construction. As a matter of fact, this corrector is an additional attenuator inserted between one of the loop windings and the receiver amplifying circuits.

# INCORPORATED DOCUMENTATION SENSE ANTENNA (Figure 6)

#### 1 - GENERAL

This aerial is used for the radio-navigational reception of the radio-compass signals.

#### 2 - CHARACTERISTICS

- Bandwidth: 190 - 1799,5 kHz.

- Radiation pattern : omnidirectional

- Antenna capacity: 50 pF.

#### 3 - DESCRIPTION

The antenna consists of a glass fiber reinforced resin fairing (1) secured on a light alloy flange (2).

The flange is drilled with five holes (3) for passing the antenna attaching screws.

The fairing is internally lined with a metal sheet (4), and externally coated with metalized paint. This paint, which has a certain resistance, forms the second plate of the antenna capacitor. It is also provided as a conducting path for discharging the electrostatic charges.

The fairing assembly is protected with a coat of CELOMER lacquer spread over the whole surface, and with a coat of PYROLAC paint over the leading edge.

The internal face of the flange is fitted with a bonding seal (5) for proper electrical contact between the flange and the aircraft structure.
